Figure 10: Unit Identification and Commitment Details 
3.10.1.2 Specifying Commitment Data 
To model the Generating Unit commitment information, enter details in the 
fields provided on the Commitment Data tab: 
• Boiler/Turbine Information 
− Minimum Online Hours: The minimum time allowed from 
synchronization to offline, in hours. 
− Minimum Offline Hours: The minimum time allowed from offline to 
synchronization, in hours. 
− Turbine Startup (MBTU): The number of MBTUs required to cold 
start the turbine. This must be a number greater than or equal to 0. 
− Boiler Startup (MBTU): The number of MBTUs required to cold 
start the boiler. This must be a number greater than or equal to 0. 
− Transition Ramp Rate (MW/Hr): The ramp rate from 
synchronization to ready-for-control at the economic dispatch 
minimum. This must be a number greater than or equal to 0. This 
entry is ignored for peakers. 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  Substation Topology 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
55
 
− Time Decay (Hr): The number of hours it takes the boiler to lose 
63% of its heat. This must be a number greater than or equal to 0. 
− Boiler Efficiency (%): The boiler efficiency factor, in percent. This 
number is divided into the incremental heat rate curve to account 
for the time since the boiler was last cleaned. A value is required in 
this field. 
− Must Run Unit? When selected, this check box specifies that the 
unit must be committed (when not on maintenance) for the Unit 
Commitment study. This is valid only for non-peakers. 
− Peaker Unit? When selected, this check box specifies that, if 
committed, the unit must be dispatched at its economic maximum. 
A peaking gas turbine (e.g., a combustion turbine) is a unit that 
counts toward total reserve even when offline, and that has a 
minimum up time of one hour or less. 
• Maintenance Information 
− Maintenance Cost per Startup ($): The cost of maintenance 
incurred by the unit each time it is synchronized, in dollars. This 
value must be greater than or equal to 0. 
− Maintenance Cost per MWH: The cost of maintenance per MWH 
of generation, in dollars per MWH. This must be greater than or 
equal to 0.0. The term “maintenance” refers to a unit’s full duration. 
This parameter becomes a term in incremental cost curve 
calculations that are used by RTGEN and STGEN, for example. A 
value is required in this field. 
− Maintenance Cost per Hour: The maintenance cost incurred each 
hour the generating unit is synchronized, in dollars. This must be 
greater than or equal to 0. A value is required in this field. 
− Startup Group: Dropdown list of startup groups. If left blank, the 
unit does not belong to a startup group, and it can be synchronized 
with any other unit at the plant. 
− Fuel Amount per Startup (MBTU): The number of MBTUs 
required to start the generating unit. This must be a number greater 
than or equal to 0. A value is required in this field for dispatchable 
units, and it is used in the startup cost model only when using 
incremental fuel rate curves. 
3.10.1.3 Specifying MW Limits 
To model the Generating Unit limits of operation, enter details in the fields 
provided on the MW Limits tab: 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  Substation Topology 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
56
 
• Reference Limits (MW) 
Note: Reference limits cannot be changed online. 
− Current Capacity (MW): The MW reference level of the generating 
unit capacity. 
− Advisory ECO Maximum (MW): The maximum MW reference 
level of the generating unit’s advisory ECO. 
− Maximum Capacity (MW): The maximum MW level allowed for 
generating unit operation (operating limit). 
− Minimum Capacity (MW): The minimum MW level at which the 
generating unit can operate (operating limit). This must be a 
number less than or equal to the LFC Minimum, and greater than or 
equal to 0. 
− LFC Maximum (MW): The MW reference level of the generating 
unit’s maximum allowed when under load frequency control. This 
must be a number greater than or equal to the ECO MAX REF, and 
less than or equal to the CAP REF. Online changes to the LFC 
MAX value are limited by this value as well. 
− LFC Minimum (MW): The MW reference level of the generating 
unit’s minimum allowed when under load frequency control. This 
must be a number less than or equal to the ECO Minimum, and 
greater than or equal to the Minimum Capacity. Online changes to 
the LFC MIN value are limited by this value as well. 
− ECO Maximum (MW): The MW reference level of the generating 
unit’s economic dispatch maximum. This must be a number greater 
than or equal to the ECO MIN REF, and less than or equal to the 
LFC MAX REF. Online changes to the ECO MAX value are limited 
by this value as well. 
− ECO Minimum (MW): The MW reference level of the generating 
unit’s economic dispatch minimum. This must be a number greater 
than or equal to the LFC MIN REF, and less than or equal to the 
ECO MAX REF. Online changes to the ECO MIN value are limited 
by this value as well. 
• Operating Limits (MW) 
Note: Operating limits can be changed online by the operator. 
− Current Capacity (MW): The default MW level of the generating 
unit’s current maximum capacity. This must be a number greater 
than or equal to the LFC MAX, and less than or equal to the MAX 
UNIT CAP. 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  Substation Topology 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
57
 
− LFC Maximum (MW): The maximum MW level allowed for the 
generating unit under load frequency control. This must be a 
number greater than or equal to the ECO MAX, and less than or 
equal to the Current Capacity. 
− LFC Minimum (MW): The minimum MW level allowed for the 
generating unit under load frequency control. This must be a 
number greater than or equal to CAP MIN, and less than or equal 
to the ECO MIN. 
− ECO Maximum (MW): The economic dispatch maximum MW level 
for the generating unit. This must be a number greater than or 
equal to the ECO MIN, and less than or equal to the LFC MAX. 
− ECO Minimum (MW): The economic dispatch minimum MW level 
for the generating unit. This must be a number greater than or 
equal to the LFC MIN, and less than or equal to the ECO MAX. 
− Advisory ECO Maximum (MW): The MW maximum reference 
level of the generating unit’s advisory ECO. This must be a number 
greater than the Advisory ECO Minimum, and less than or equal to 
the Current Capacity. 
− Advisory ECO Minimum (MW): The MW minimum reference level 
of the generating unit’s advisory ECO. This must be a number less 
than the Advisory ECO Maximum, and greater than or equal to 
CAP MIN. 
− Unit Offline Threshold (% Maximum Capacity): Specify the 
offline threshold as a percent of the unit’s total capacity. Default 
value: 0.01. 
− Unit Offline Threshold Deadband (%): Specify the offline 
deadband as a percent of the offline limit. Default value: 0.1. 
• General Information 
− Max Spinning Reserve (MW): The MW level of the maximum 
allowed spinning reserve from this generating unit. This must be a 
number greater than or equal to 0. 
− Raise Wind-Up Limit (MW): The generating unit’s wind-up limit in 
the up direction. The wind-up limit is the maximum allowed 
difference between the setpoint for the unit and the current 
generation. 
Note: Toggle the Count for Spinning Reserve When Offline check 
box or the Count for Operating Reserve When Offline check box 
ON, to activate this field for data entry. 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  Substation Topology 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
58
 
− Lower Wind-Up Limit (MW): The generating unit’s wind-up limit in 
the down direction. The wind-up limit is the maximum allowed 
difference between the setpoint for the unit and the current 
generation. 
Note: Toggle the Count for Spinning Reserve When Offline check 
box or the Count for Operating Reserve When Offline check box 
ON, to activate this field for data entry. 
− Unit Limit Deadband: The percentage of the alarm limit for the 
deadband to issue a return-to-normal message after a unit MW 
change alarm in AGC. 
− Frequency Deadband: Allowable deviation of the unit frequency, 
as defined in GENMODEL. 
− Count for Spinning Reserve When Offline? When selected, this 
check box specifies that the unit counts toward spinning reserve 
when offline and not on maintenance. This field is valid only for 
peaking units. 
− Count for Operating Reserve When Offline? When selected, this 
check box specifies that the unit counts toward operating reserve 
when offline and not on maintenance. 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  Substation Topology 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
59

3.10.1.4 Specifying Ramp Rate Settings 
To model the Generating Unit ramp rate information, enter details in the 
fields provided on the Ramp Rates tab: 
• Ramp Rate Settings 
− Set Short-Term to Nominal Rates? When selected, this check 
box specifies that the short-term response rates are set equal to the 
nominal rates. 
− Set Down Rate to Up Rate? When selected, this check box 
specifies that the down response rates are set equal to the up 
rates. 
− Nominal Up Rate (MW/Min): The unit’s default nominal (MSR) 
response rate (MW/MIN) in the UP direction. This must be a 
number greater than or equal to 0.0, and less than or equal to the 
value entered in the Short-Term Up Rate field. 
− Nominal Down Rate (MW/Min): The unit’s default nominal (MSR) 
response rate (MW/MIN) in the DOWN direction. This must be a 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  Substation Topology 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
60
 
number greater than or equal to 0.0, and less than or equal to the 
value entered in the Short-Term Down Rate field. 
− Short-Term Up Rate (MW/Min): The number of MWs per minute 
the unit is capable of producing at faster, short-term up ramp rates. 
The values indicate the unit’s energy storage capacity for upward 
ramping. 
− Short-Term Down Rate (MW/Min): The number of MWs per 
minute the unit is capable of producing at faster, short-term down 
ramp rates. The values indicate the unit’s energy absorbing 
capacity for downward ramping. 
• Maximum MW Change Using Short-Term Ramp Rate 
− Up Rate (MW): The maximum permissible change in unit output 
(MW) at the short-term rate in the UP (raise) direction. This must be 
a number greater than or equal to 1 (3% of unit capacity). 
− Down Rate (MW): The maximum permissible change of unit output 
(MW) at the short-term rate in the DOWN (lower) direction. This 
must be a number greater than or equal to 1 (3% of unit capacity).

The Forbidden Zones tab is used to set high and low forbidden zone limits 
for a generating unit. “Forbidden zones” are MW output regions 
considered uneconomic or undesirable for operating purposes. 
Each unit can have more than one forbidden zone, provided that the limits 
for the zone are not telemetered. Only one set of forbidden zone limits can 
be telemetered for each unit.

3.11 Configuring Nodes and Connecting Terminals 
Nodes model a junction point between network elements, and identify the 
ends of branches. Each kind of branch has a “double entry” record to 
describe each end. These records are used in network searching 
algorithms, and they are vital to the process of linking flows in branches 
onto one-line diagrams. 
Nodes are modeled after the creation of various components, such as 
circuit breakers, DC converters, generators and generating units, loads, 
and shunt capacitors and reactors. 
The following procedure assumes that you have already created the 
component for which you are modeling nodes. To define nodes for a 
component, complete the following steps: 
1. Click the Topology toolbar button. 
2. Navigate to the Equipment Group fo r the substation where you want to 
connect the component, and expand it. 
3. If required, define st ation node parameters using the Substation form. 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  Substation Topology 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
80
 
4. Drag and drop the component under the appropriate station node in 
the navigation tree (left pane). 
5. Define connectivity between equi pment using the Network Topology 
tree view. 
6. Select the Remove Unused Topo logy Nodes option from the Tools 
menu. 
This option, enabled only in the Network Topology view, removes any 
equipment nodes that were automatically generated during component 
creation, but are not required for connectivity. 
 
 
Figure 25: Terminal Connections 
3.12 Specifying the Equipment Type of a Winding End 
The e-terramodeler Topology Helper facility automatically creates terminal 
ends at the time a branch end such as a transformer winding end, an AC 
line end, or a zero impedance branch end is created. However, 
e-terramodeler does not assign an equipment type for these terminal 
ends; thus, it does not create a corresponding SCADA device. Without a 
device record, the database builder cannot define measurements at the 
branch end. 
To create the SCADA device for a transformer winding end, the model 
builder must specify the Equipment Type of the winding end, toggle the 
SCADA check box ON, and save the winding end definition. 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  Substation Topology 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
81
 
e-terramodeler will then create a terminal device corresponding to the 
winding end. 
The database builder can now add a Measurement group and any of the 
data point types offered under the equipment’s Measurement group folder. 
3.13 Load Model Components 
The load model has the following components: 
• Loads: Describe how much real and reactive power is extracted at the 
nodes (or buses). 
• Load Types: Determine the effects off-nominal frequency and voltage 
have on the amount of load extracted. Load types are optional. 
• Load Areas: Distribute the total power to the individual loads. 
Functionally, they separate loads into groups and subgroups with 
similar scheduling patterns. 
Every load area must belong to a schedule. The schedule can specify 
a constant value or a different value for every minute of the week. The 
schedule of a parent load area affects the amount of load assigned to 
its children and, to a lesser degree, the amount of load assigned to its 
sibling load areas. 
RTNET updates the schedules of all load areas that are 
measurement-observable. Measurement-observable load areas have a 
user-determined percentage of load that is measured directly from 
SCADA. 
− Top-Level (Forecast) Load Areas: Are a parent to other load 
areas. Consequently, base load and parent fraction information has 
a different meaning in Forecast Load Areas than in other load 
areas. 
− Load Schedules: Are assigned to load areas. They contain 
time-variable base loads (minimum loads), and an assigned fraction 
of the parent’s total load. 
− Load VAr Schedules: Are assigned to individual loads. They 
contain time-variable base MVAr loads (minimum MVAr loads), as 
well as the power factor of the load. The power factor is used to 
determine the MVAr load, in addition to the base, from the MW 
load. The sum of the two components yields the total MVAr load. 
− Base Load: The minimum load distributed to that load branch. In 
top-level load areas (forecast load areas), this is the total load+loss 
for an entire load tree. Base loads can be entered manually, or 
updated via RTNET in study mode or real time. 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  Substation Topology 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
82
 
− Parent Fractions: Are fields in the load area schedules. 
− Load Fractions: Distribution factor assigned to loads. The Load 
Fraction determines the amount of load above the base load that is 
distributed to that branch of the load model hierarchy. In top-level 
load areas, this is the fraction of the total load+loss that represents 
the total load for the tree. Load fractions can be entered manually, 
or updated via RTNET in study mode or real time. 
3.14 Defining Loads 
The load model enables an accurate representation of the flow of power 
through the network by describing how power is extracted from the 
network. 
The simplest way to model a load is to start with one load record, and then 
use schedules to vary the modeled load over time. This simple method is 
possible only if you assign a top-level load area to every load. By adding a 
few hierarchical layers to the model structure, you can increase the total 
number of schedules that are required. This makes it easier to modify the 
total load, and it expedites load forecasting based on the predicted 
weather and expected load. 
The load model contains a structure that is related to, but separate from, 
the topology hierarchy. The load model is used by all the network 
applications that solve for a network state, but it interacts most strongly 
with RTNET and e-terrasimulator. RTNET updates the load forecast 
schedules by processing raw data from SCADA. e-terrasimulator uses the 
load model, not only to obtain a state solution, but also for modeling 
loadshed. 
To define feeder loads at a substation, complete the following steps: 
1. Click the Equipment  toolbar button. 
2. Define voltage level information. 
a. Navigate to the Equipment Gr oups folder for the appropriate 
substation, and expand it. 
i. To add a new equipment group record, place the cursor on the 
Equipment Groups folder, click the right mouse button, and 
select the Add Equipment Group option. 
ii. To update an existing equipment group record, select its icon. 
b. Define voltage level information using the Equipment Group form. 
3. Define load information. 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  Substation Topology 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
83
 
a. Navigate to the Power Equipm ent folder for the appropriate 
substation, and expand it. 
b. Expand the Loads folder to expose its contents. 
i. To add a new load record, place the cursor on the Loads folder, 
click the right mouse button, and select the Add Load option. 
ii. To update an existing load record, select its icon. 
c. Define load information using the Load form. 
4. Define topology as described in Configuring Nodes and Connecting 
Terminals. 
5. Map the load to the appropriate  SCADA measurements and controls.

3.16 Defining Circuit Breakers 
Switching devices are typically circuit breakers (CBs), but they can also be 
line switches, motor-operated disconnects, or other circuit-breaking 
devices. 
The common electrical attribute of all such elements is that they are either 
open (infinite impedance) or closed (zero impedance). Each such device 
is a branch whose relationship to network components is identified by its 
two terminal branches. 
Each circuit breaker is defined within a substation. The name of a CB 
must be unique within the same device type in a substation. A breaker 
schedule can be assigned to a circuit breaker if it has a schedule of 
operation that is a function of time. 
To define circuit breakers for a substation, complete the following steps: 
1. Click the Equipment  toolbar button. 
2. Define voltage level information. 
a. Navigate to the Equipment Gr oups folder for the appropriate 
substation, and expand it. 
i. To add a new equipment group re cord, place the cursor on the 
Equipment Groups folder, click the right mouse button, and 
select the Add Equipment Group option. 
ii. To update an existing equipment group record, select its icon. 
b. Define voltage level information using the Equipment Group form. 
3. Define circuit breaker information. 
a. Navigate to the Power Equipment folder for the appropriate 
substation, and expand it. 
b. Expand the Switching Devices fo lder to expose its contents. 
i. To add a new circuit breaker re cord, place the cursor on the 
Switching Devices folder, click the right mouse button, and 
select the Add Switching Device option. 
ii. To update an existing circuit breaker record, select its icon. 
c. Define circuit breaker information using the Switching Device form. 
4. Define topology as described in Configuring Nodes and Connecting 
Terminals. 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  Substation Topology 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide

5. Map the circuit breaker to t he appropriate SCADA measurements and

3.18 Defining Transformers 
The transformer model includes the name, the “from and to” AC nodes, 
LTC and FIX taps, tap types, voltage regulation schedule, voltage or flow 
sensitivity, and pi model impedance parameters. Transformers are 
represented as sets of two-winding transformers. Each set of two-winding 
transformers is associated with a physical transformer. 
The tap connected to the first node is allowed to change position under 
load. This tap is called either the “LTC tap” (for “Load Tap Changing”) or 
the “TCUL tap” (for “Tap Changing Under Load”). The remaining tap can 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  Substation Topology 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
86
 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  Substation Topology 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
87
only change positions when the transformer is removed from service, and 
it is referred to as the “FIX tap”. 
If the transformer regulates a voltage, the transformer record contains a 
reference to a regulation schedule. The schedule describes when the 
regulation is to take place and the voltage range. 
The tap types referenced by the transformer model specify the tap limits 
and physical time delay characteristics for regulation purposes. 
XF
XF21 
XF2h
XFMR2

3.19.1.1 Specifying Winding Parameters 
To specify transformer winding terminal nominal voltages, tap settings, 
and other operating information, enter details in the fields provided on the 
General Information tab: 
• Fixed Tap Equipment Group: Dropdown list of fixed tap equipment 
groups. 
• LTC Equipment Group: Dropdown list of LTC equipment groups. 
• Winding Parameters (% on a 100-MVA base) 
− Series Resistance: The transformer’s series resistance, in percent 
on a 100-MVA base. This field requires a value. If left blank, it will 
result in a validation error. 
− Series Reactance: The transformer’s series reactance, in percent 
on a 100-MVA base. This field requires a non-zero value. If left 
blank, it will result in a validation error. 
− Knee Point on the Magnetization Curve: The point on the 
transformer magnetization curve corresponding to flux where the 
transformer begins to saturate, in p.u. Volt/p.u. Hertz. The default 
is 1.3. 
− Shunt Magnetizing Conductance: The shunt magnetizing 
conductance of the transformer, in percent on a 100-MVA base. 
The default is 0. 
− Shunt Magnetizing Susceptance: The shunt magnetizing 
susceptance of the transformer, in percent on a 100-MVA base. 
The default is 0. Typically, any non-zero value entered in this field 
should be entered as a negative number. 
− Slope of the Magnetization Curve: The slope of the transformer 
magnetization curve in the saturation region (the shunt magnetizing 
susceptance of the transformer operating in the saturation region), 
in percent on a 100-MVA base. The default is 50 times the value of 
the shunt magnetizing susceptance. 
• Tap Settings 
− Fixed Nominal Voltage: The nominal voltage of the transformer at 
the fixed tap side, in Kilovolts. 
− Fixed Normal Tap: The normal tap at the fixed tap side (“To” side). 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  Substation Topology 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
89
 
− Fixed Tap Changer Type: Dropdown list of tap changer types. 
Note: To add, modify, or browse a tap changer type in the 
displayed list, use the Tap Changer Types table. Click the 
Ellipsis (...) button to access this table. 
− LTC Nominal Voltage: The nominal voltage of the LTC tap, in 
Kilovolts. 
− LTC Normal Tap: The normal LTC tap at the “From” side. 
− LTC Tap Changer Type: Dropdown list of tap changer types. 
Note: To add, modify, or browse a tap changer type in the 
displayed list, use the Tap Changer Types table. Click the 
Ellipsis (...) button to access this table. 
• Voltage Regulation 
− Voltage Sensitivity: The voltage change with regard to the MVAr 
change for the device to regulate the bus voltage, p.u. Volt/MVAr. 
This field should not be left blank if the regulation node data exists. 
You can run different powerflow solutions with and without this 
capacitor to determine its voltage sensitivity. 
− Regulating Node: Dropdown list of regulating nodes. 
− Regulating Schedule: Dropdown list of regulating schedules. This 
field can be left blank. 
Note: To add, modify, or browse a regulating schedule in the 
displayed list, use the Network Schedules. Click the Ellipsis (...) 
button to access these schedules. 
− Enable Automatic Voltage Regulation: When selected, this 
check box allows automatic voltage regulation. 
− Enable Load-Based Voltage Regulation: A check mark specifies 
that load-based voltage regulation for the transformer will be 
enabled. 
− Load-Based Regulating Schedule: Dropdown list of regulating 
schedules. This field can be left blank. 
• Auto-Transformer 
− Master: Dropdown list of master transformers for this winding. This 
field must be blank if the XF is an auto-transformer master, or is not 
part of an auto-transformer model. 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  Substation Topology 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
90
 
− Master? When selected, this check box indicates a master phase 
shifter. 
− Follower? When selected, this check box indicates a follower 
phase shifter.

3.19.1.5 Transformer Tap Ratio Tables 
In certain situations, such as for three-winding transformers, an unusual 
relationship between tap setting and voltage tap ratio may exist. For these 
cases, tap ratio tables are assigned to transformers indirectly by assigning 
them to tap types. 
If a tap type is assigned to a tap ratio table, then transformers assigned to 
that tap type have their voltage tap ratios calculated using the table. If no 
table is assigned to a tap type, then transformers assigned to that tap type 
have their voltage tap ratios calculated in the usual manner. 
To define a transformer tap ratio table, complete the following steps: 
1. Select the Unit and Transformer Tables option on the View menu. 
This opens the Auxiliary Tables window to the Unit and Transformer 
Tables folder, and opens it to expose the table type folders. 
2. Right-click on the Unit and Transfo rmer Tables folder and choose the 
Add TAB option from the menu that appears. 
3. Add one TABPT record for each point on the curve where the slope of 
the curve changes. If the curve is linear, define two points. 
4. Enter the x coordinate of each TABPT, in terms of transformer tap 
position. 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  Substation Topology 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
93
 
This will add a series of rows to the Tap Positions grid. 
5. Add one TABVAL record for each TABPT record. 
6. Enter the per-unit voltage tap ratio in the row provided in the Tap Ratio 
Correction Table grid. 
7. Save the table. 
To assign a transformer tap ratio table to a tap changer type, complete the 
following steps: 
1. Call up the Tap Changer Types table using the Reference Table 
window. 
2. Locate the tap changer type of interest. 
3. Assign the tap ratio correction ta ble to the tap changer type using the 
Tap Ratio Correction Table cell on the grid. 
To assign a transformer tap ratio table to a transformer, complete the 
following steps: 
1. Call up the transformer’s Transformer Winding form. 
2. Locate the Tap Settings frame on the General Information tab. 
3. Click the dropdown menu button in t he tap type field (Fixed or LTC). 
4. Select the tap type whose assign ed tap ratio correction table matches 
your specification. 
5. Save your work. 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  Substation Topology 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
94
 
 
 
Figure 30: Tap Ratio Definition Table 
3.19.1.6 Phase Shifter Correction Tables 
The transformer can also control the angle across it instead of, or in 
addition to, the voltage magnitude. Phase shifter data under a transformer 
model is used to specify the LTC tap angle. 
Note: The LTC tap of phase shifter is different from the LTC tap of 
transformer, so the magnitude and angle of each LTC tap can be adjusted 
independently. Thus, when both voltage and MW flow are being regulated, 
two physically different taps are present on the same transformer. As 
before, a regulation schedule is specified, which describes the time and 
range of MW flow regulation. 
The transformer/phase shifter impedance can optionally be modeled as a 
function of the phase shifter’s angle, in degrees. The correction table for 
phase-shifter impedance is referenced in the phase shifter’s tap type. The 
correction table gives X and corresponding Y values, where X is the phase 
shift in degrees, and Y is multiplied by a phase shifter’s nominal 
impedance to get the corrected impedance. 
Network applications that move phase shift taps will recalculate 
impedance based on the correction tables, as needed. 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  Substation Topology 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
95
 
If an impedance correction table is not referenced, the phase shifter’s 
impedance is assumed to be constant. 
 
 
Figure 31: Phase Shifter Impedance Table 
3.20 Defining AC Transmission Lines 
Each AC Line is multi-terminal and can contain one or more two-terminal 
line segments. Each end of each line segment must be assigned to a 
node within a station. Each line segment is distinguished by its parent line 
identifier, plus its own identifier. The name of the line identifier must be 
unique, and the name of the line segment identifier must be unique under 
its parent line. 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  Substation Topology 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
96
 
 
 
Figure 32: AC Line Segment Definition Form 
The line segment model is the classic pi-model. It consists of an 
admittance — G (line conductance) + jB (line susceptance) — connecting 
two nodes, and a susceptance of 0.5 Bch (line charging susceptance) 
between each node and ground representing the line charging. 
The admittance is directly calculated from the R and X input parameters: 
G = 10,000R/(R² + X²) 
B = -10,000X/(R² + X²) 
where: 
• 10,000 = The factor that converts  the impedances, entered in percent 
on a 100-MVA base, to the admittances that are in PU on a 1-MVA 
base. 
• R = The line resistance in percent. 
• X = The line reactance in percent. 
The power flow from node i to node j, measured at node i, is given for a 
general pi-model by: 
Wi = |Vi|²(Goi + G) – |Vi||Vj|[Gcos(Ai – Aj) + Bsin(Ai 
– Aj)] 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  Substation Topology 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
97
 
Ri = -|Vi|²(Boi + B) – |Vi||Vj|[Gsin(Ai – Aj) – Bcos(Ai 
– Aj)] 
where: 
• Vi, Ai = The magnitude and the angle of the voltage at node i. 
• Vj, Aj = The magnitude and the angle of the voltage at node j. 
• G, B = The conductance and susceptance of the line connecting i and 
j. 
• Goi, Boi = The conductance and susceptance of the pi-leg to ground at 
node i. 
− Note: These values cannot be NULL. 
• Wi, Ri = The real and reactive powe r flowing into the line from node i. 
For a transmission line, Goi = 0.0, and Boi = 0.5Bch (Bch = the line 
charging susceptance). For a series capacitor or reactor modeled as a 
transmission line, G = 0.0. 
To specify an AC Transmission Line using the pop-up operations menus 
in the Transmission Line view, complete these steps: 
1. Click the Line toolbar button. 
2. Navigate to the appropriate operating division in the Transmission 
Lines folder, and expand it. 
3. Expand the AC Transmission Lines fo lder to reveal its contents. 
a. To add a new AC Transmission Line record, place the cursor on the 
AC Transmission Lines folder, click the right mouse button, and 
select the Add AC Transmission Line option. 
Note: To update an existing AC Transmission Line record, select 
its icon. 
4. Specify transmission line operating information about the AC 
Transmission Line form. 
5. Define each line segment using the right mouse button and the Add 
AC Line Segment option. 
6. Specify AC Line Segment info rmation about the AC Line Segment 
form. 
Note: Even though the AC Line Ends (Near and Far) have been 
inserted automatically, the model builder must manually enter the Near 
node, Near Station, Far node, and Far Station values. 
7. If this branch end is measured, specify the branch end Equipment 
Type, and set the SCADA check box on the Data Transfer tab ON. 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  Substation Topology 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
98
 
When the modeler saves the AC Transmission Line record, the 
modeling system propagates the branch end to the SCADA database 
as a SCADA DEVICE record. 
8. Specify branch end measurements  using the Add MEAS option and 
the data point add structures. Specify AC Line End measurements and 
controls. 
9. Save your work. 
 
 
Figure 33: AC Line Segment Definition

3.22 Defining the Line Ends 
To define AC Line End data for the new Line End records (DEVTYP and

Proprietary – See Copyright Page  Substation Topology 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
100

− Shunt Reactive Devices

− Transformers

3.23 Specifying the Equipment Type of AC Line Ends and 
ZBRs 
The e-terramodeler Topology Helper facility automatically creates terminal 
ends at the time a branch end such as a transformer winding end, an AC 
line end, or a zero impedance branch end is created. However, 
e-terramodeler does not assign an equipment type for these terminal 
ends; thus, it doesn’t create a corresponding SCADA device. Without a 
device record, the database builder cannot define measurements at the 
branch end. 
To create the SCADA device for an AC Line End or Zero Impedance 
Branch End, the model builder must specify the Equipment Type of the 
terminal end, toggle the SCADA check box ON, and save the terminal end 
definition. e-terramodeler creates a terminal device corresponding to the 
winding end. 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  Substation Topology 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
101
 
The database builder can now add a Measurement group and any of the 
data point types offered under the equipment’s Measurement group folder. 
3.24 Defining Shunt Reactive Devices 
A shunt capacitor or reactor is defined by whether it injects MVAr into the 
transmission system or extracts MVAr. The shunt’s susceptance 
parameter is converted to the shunt device’s MVAr parameter based on 
the device’s nominal voltage. 
A shunt capacitor or reactor can be defined as a switching shunt device 
and, in this way, regulate bus voltage. A switching breaker can be 
assigned to a switching shunt device. If no regulating switch is assigned, 
the switching shunt device is considered to be directly connected to or 
disconnected from the network during the bus voltage regulation 
operation. Also, an associated voltage regulation schedule should be 
assigned to the switching shunt device for providing the target and 
deviation of regulation voltage. 
To define shunt reactive devices for a substation, complete the following 
steps: 
1. Click the Equipment  toolbar button. 
2. Define voltage level information. 
a. Navigate to the Equipment Gr oups folder for the appropriate 
substation, and expand it. 
i. To add a new equipment group record, place the cursor on the 
Equipment Groups folder, click the right mouse button, and 
select the Add Equipment Group option. 
ii. To update an existing equipment group record, select its icon. 
b. Define voltage level information using the Equipment Group form. 
3. Define shunt reactive devices. 
a. Navigate to the Power Equipment folder for the appropriate 
substation, and expand it. 
b. Expand the Shunt Reactive Device s folder to expose its contents. 
i. To add a new shunt reactive device record, place the cursor on 
the Shunt Reactive Devices folder, click the right mouse button, 
and select the Add Shunt Reactive Device option. 
ii. To update an existing shunt reac tive device record, select its 
icon. 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  Substation Topology 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
102
 
4. Define shunt capacitor and reactor information using the Shunt 
Reactive Device form. 
5. Define topology as described in Configuring Nodes and Connecting 
Terminals. 
 
 
Figure 34: Capacitor/Reactor Definition

3.27 Defining Auxiliary Loads 
An Auxiliary Load definition represents station service load, and it is used 
to model special load at a power plant. An auxiliary load is always 
associated with a generating unit, and its MW load is a function of the unit 
MW generation. ModelerUI provides an option for excluding the auxiliary 
load from the total company load summary. 
Parameters used to define an auxiliary load model include its name, the 
terminal node name, load type, unit voltage limit table name, unit load 
table name, and voltage limit set name. 
Auxiliary power is presumed to be related to the amount of power currently 
being generated at the station, instead of to the demand in general. 
Auxiliary power need not be defined in terms of net generation. 
To define an auxiliary load, complete the following steps: 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  Substation Topology 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
104
 
1. Click the Equipment  toolbar button. 
2. Define voltage level information. 
a. Navigate to the Equipment Gr oups folder for the appropriate 
substation, and expand it. 
i. To add a new equipment group re cord, place the cursor on the 
Equipment Groups folder, click the right mouse button, and 
select the Add Equipment Group option. 
b. To update an existing equipment group record, select its icon. 
c. Define voltage level information using the Equipment Group form. 
3. Define auxiliary loads. 
a. Navigate to the Power Equipm ent folder for the appropriate 
substation, and expand it. 
b. Expand the Auxiliary Loads folder to expose its contents. 
i. To add a new auxiliary load re cord, place the cursor on the 
Auxiliary Loads folder, click the right mouse button, and select 
the Add Auxiliary Load option. 
ii. To update an existing auxiliary load record, select its icon. 
4. Define auxiliary loads using the Auxiliary Loads form. 
5. Define topology as described in Configuring Nodes and Connecting 
Terminals.

4. Power Equipment Monitoring and Control 
The substation topology definition represents the deployment of power 
equipment at substations. Measurement structures and device controller 
information reside in the substation topology definition. For example, when 
defining the deployment of a generating unit at a station, the controller 
(PLC) that sends pulses or setpoints to the generator is also defined. The 
MW flow analogs and the plant controller setpoint values and control point 
assignments (Auto/Manual) will reside in the generating unit and plant 
controller definitions, respectively. 
The statuses, counts, and analogs retrieved from monitored equipment 
are stored in a measurement structure whose root is the MEAS record and 
whose parent records are the DEVICE records. 
The records that complete the measurement structure for data retrieval 
and supervisory control are the ANALOG, ALGLIM, SETPNT, POINT, 
CTRL, and COUNT records. 
The records that reside in the measurement structure for computational 
purposes are the SCADA Constant (CONST), Calculation Function 
(FUNC), Function Argument (ARG) records, and the Data Point Reference 
records. 
4.1 Defining Measurement Groups 
Measurement Group (MEAS) records are used to organize data under 
DEVICE records. The MEAS record structure provides store locations for 
telemetered and computed data. Measurement Group records are not part 
of a measurement’s unique identification (composite key). 
The figures below illustrate the flexibility of the measurement structure. 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  Power Equipment Monitoring and Control 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
107

Note: Measurement Group records often contain the information 
necessary to carry out special calculations or special control sequences. 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  Power Equipment Monitoring and Control 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
108
 
 
 
Figure 38: Computational Measurement Structure 
To define measurement groups for devices in the substation topology, 
complete the following steps: 
1. Click the Equipment  toolbar button. 
2. Navigate to the appropriate substation icon, and expand it. 
3. Expand the device or equipment fo lder to expose its contents. 
a. To define a new measurement group, place the cursor on the 
appropriate device or equipment icon, click the right mouse button, 
and select the Add MEAS option. 
b. To update an existing measurem ent group, select its icon. 
4. Define measurement group informat ion using the Measurement Group 
form. 
5. Within the measurement group, model one or more data points and 
calculation references using the folders provided. 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  Power Equipment Monitoring and Control 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
109
 
4.2 Defining Data Points for Device Monitoring and Control 
Data point definitions specify memory locations for data telemetered to 
and from devices in the field. Each data point is device-specific and is 
identified by composite key. The composite key is a concatenation of the 
SUBSTN, DEVTYP, DEVICE, and <data point> identifiers. This string 
provides the unique search path to the location of a telemetered or 
measurement system value. 
The device-specific data points mentioned above are stored on the 
ANALOG, ALGLIM, SETPNT, POINT, CTRL, and COUNT records 
mentioned previously. 
Note: e-terrascada engineers also utilize the flexibility of the SCADA data 
retrieval system and measurement structure to store calculated values; 
SCADA constants; and analog, accumulator, status, setpoint, and control 
references. These “engineering values” co-mingle with the real-time data 
without interfering with SCADA operations. 
4.3 Defining Count Accumulator Inputs 
Count accumulator records are used to model RTU measurements 
accumulated over a discrete period of time. Disposition information may 
be entered for each count accumulator record, such as whether it should 
be recorded for Historical Data Recording. 
To define count accumulator inputs for substation equipment, complete 
the following steps:

4.4 Defining Analog Inputs 
Analog records model non-discrete analog measurements in the power 
system, such as voltages, megavars, etc. An analog can be a scanned 
value from an RTU, a local input, or a remote site, or it can be an 
untelemetered value or a calculated analog value. 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  Power Equipment Monitoring and Control

Proprietary – See Copyright Page  Power Equipment Monitoring and Control 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide

Figure 41: Unit Analog Definition 
Analog Input records may have associated Analog Limit records that 
specify the high and low bounds for the analog record’s Alarms. The 
Analog Limit record contains the actual limit values, as well as the alarm 
deadband, which prevents Alarm “chatter” when the Analog value 
oscillates between the high and low limits. Separate alarm categories can 
be defined for each Analog Limit record, allowing the Analog to transition 
to different alarm categories when it violates more-severe limits.

4.4.1.1 Defining Analog Limits 
ALTLIM records store alternate high and low values for analog limits. They 
are organized in sets as children of LIMIT records, and they are identified 
by an Alternate Limit Set name that exists in the ALTSET naming table (a 
ModelerUI Reference Table). 
 
 
Figure 43: Analog Limit Definition 
4.4.1.2 Defining Setpoint Controls (Analog Outputs) 
Setpoint controls are modeled under Analog Input records and are used to 
send numeric values to RTUs or local I/O devices. The Setpoint record 
defines what actions to take when alarms are inhibited. Alarms are issued 
on the Setpoint record when an Analog value receives a supervisory 
control but fails to reach the expected state within a configured amount of 
time. A deadband can be used to define how close the analog value must 
come to the expected setpoint value before assuming that the supervisory 
action was successful. 
To define setpoint controls for substation equipment, complete the 
following steps:

4.5 Defining Status Inputs 
Status Input records are used to model discrete status measurements in 
the power system, such as OPEN or CLOSE, AUTO or MANUAL device 
states. A Status Input record can also be configured to model a three-state 
measurement, such as “Open”, “Closed”, or “In-Transit”. Status Input 
records can also include information specifying the action to be performed 
when alarms are inhibited, whether multiple change detection (MCD) 
processing is to be performed for the status point, whether the status point 
is included in Historical Data Recording, etc. 
Status Input records can be used to model switching devices, such as 
breakers or disconnects, in the SCADA topology model. Non-switching 
devices, such as Lines, Generators, Transformers, etc. are modeled on 
Device records. 
To define a status point for substation equipment, complete the following 
steps:

4.5.1.1 Defining Digital Controls 
Digital control records are used to model supervisory control actions on a 
device, which usually causes a change in state of the POINT record 
associated with the control. Digital control records are also used to model 
Raise/Lower commands, which require both a direction and “Pulse Width” 
to complete the supervisory control action. 
Additional information includes the action to be taken when alarms are 
inhibited on the Control record. Alarms are issued when a Status Point 
receives a supervisory control but fails to reach the expected state within a 
configured amount of time. 
Digital Controls are modeled under Status Input records. 
To define Digital Controls for substation equipment, complete the following

4.9 Defining Control Sequences 
MEAS records can also be used to define sequencing of control events. 
The sections and illustrations below provide information about the MEAS 
structures that support the Simultaneous Trip/Close Control function (type 
MBO1) and the Multiple Trip/Close function (type MBO2). 
Note: For control sequences, no matching FUNC record is required. 
4.9.1.1 Simultaneous Control Function 
In the simultaneous control function, the operator executes a control 
command sequence on a single real point and control relays, which, in the 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  Power Equipment Monitoring and Control 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
127
 
field, causes multiple simultaneous operation of several control points. In 
order to confirm control completion of all points, a MEAS structure similar 
to that illustrated in Figure 54 below is required. 
In a simultaneous sequence, the following occurs: 
1. The first CTLREF under the ME AS points to the master CTRL. 
2. The master CTRL is issued, resulting in several controls being 
executed in the power system. 
3. The other CTLREFs under the M EAS point to dummy CTRLs, which 
are never issued, but their parent POINT changes when the associated 
control in the field is executed. 
 
 
Figure 54: Simultaneous Breaker Processing 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  Power Equipment Monitoring and Control 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
128

The processing of transformer taps, automatically selected based on a 
reference voltage, is implemented using a simultaneous control structure. 
An example structure appears in Figure 55. 
 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  Power Equipment Monitoring and Control 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide

4.9.1.4 Multiple Breaker Operation 
In the multiple control function, a control command sequence is executed 
on a single dummy point by the operator. The software then issues control 
command sequences to multiple points. 
The structure illustrated in Figure 57 is an example of a multiple breaker 
operation. The CTRL flag in the MEAS record must be set ON, and the 
TYPE field of the MEAS record must be “MBO2”. 
In a multiple control sequence, the following occurs: 
1. The first CTLREF record is a placeholder, and the master CTRL is 
never issued. 
2. The CTRLs pointed to by subsequ ent CTLREF records are issued in 
sequence. Each control has an expected value set. 
3. The POINT parent of each CTRL reflects the change resulting from 
issuing the control. 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  Power Equipment Monitoring and Control 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
131
 
 
SUBSTN = PIRI 
 DEVTYP = CB 
 DEVICE = BREAKER_1 
 MEAS = CTRL 
 POINT = STAT 
  CTRL(1) = TRIP 
  CTRL(2) = CLOS 
 DEVICE = BREAKER_2 
 MEAS = CTRL 
 POINT = STAT 
  CTRL(3) = TRIP 
  CTRL(4) = CLOS 
 DEVICE = MASTER  DUMMY 
 MEAS = CTRL  DUMMY

  CTRL(5) = TRIP DUMMY 
  CTRL(6) = CLOS DUMMY

 MEAS = MBO2 
 CTLREF = 6  (MASTER CLOSE – NOT REALLY CONTROLLED)

 CTLREF = 4  (BREAKER_2 CLOSE) 
 
Figure 57: Multiple Breaker Processing 
If any breaker (or breakers) within a group has control inhibited by a tag or 
by other means, group control will still be allowed. However, the control of 
any individual breaker inhibited due to Tagging or some other reason will 
not be activated. If the Area of Responsibility of a device in a multiple 
breaker control group is not assigned to the console attempting control of 
the group, then control of such breakers will be inhibited. 
It is possible to individually control any breaker that belongs to one or 
more groups. 
4.9.1.5 Defining a Simultaneous Control Sequence 
To define a simultaneous control sequence, complete these steps: 
1. Insert the MEAS record, and nam e it for easy identification. 
“MBO1” might be a good name. 
2. Set the MEAS Type field to “Control Sequence”. 
3. Set the Sequence Name field to “Simultaneous Control” (MBO1). 
Note: Selecting the Simultaneous Control option establishes that the 
record structure being modeled will support the operation of controls 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  Power Equipment Monitoring and Control 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
132
 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  Power Equipment Monitoring and Control 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
133
from one control relay. The POINT, CTRL, and RELAY records must 
already be in place. 
4. Select the Digital Control Reference folder and select Add CTLREF, to 
insert one CTLREF record for each CTRL record that has been 
modeled. 
a. Assign the first CTLREF record to the master CTRL record, and set 
this CTLREF record’s Master Control toggle ON using the SCADA 
Control References table. 
Note: The master CTRL record is the one with the RELAY card 
assignment. 
b. Assign the other CTLREF record s to their dummy CTRL record. 
4.9.1.6 Defining a Multiple Control Sequence 
To define a multiple control sequence, complete these steps: 
1. Insert the MEAS record, and nam e it for easy identification. 
“MBO2” might be a good name. 
2. Set the MEAS Type field to “Control Sequence”. 
3. Set the Sequence Name field to  “Multiple Controls” (MBO2)”. 
Note: Selecting the Control Sequence option establishes that the 
record structure being defined will support the operation of controls 
from multiple control relays. The POINT, CTRL, and RELAY records 
must already be in place. 
4. Select the Digital Control Reference folder and select Add CTLREF, to 
insert one CTLREF record for each CTRL record that has been 
defined. 
a. Assign the first CTLREF record to the dummy master CTRL record, 
and leave this CTLREF record’s Master Control toggle set to OFF. 
Note: The master CTRL of a multiple sequence cannot be a 
member of any other control sequence. However, the master of a 
simultaneous sequence is allowed to be a member of a multiple 
sequence. 
b. Assign the other CTLREF records to CTRL records that model 
control of real devices in the field. 
Note: Each of the referenced CTRL records is one with a RELAY 
card assignment that supports a control operation. 
 
 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  Remote Terminal Unit (RTU) Definition 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
134
5. Remote Terminal Unit (RTU) Definition 
The RTU data structure is made up of Scan Group (ADRS) definitions and 
CARD definitions. The type of data stored on the card determines the 
category that the CARD definition belongs in. Any RTU-specific protocol 
details are also defined in the RTU data structure. 
5.1 Data Structure of the SCADA Telemetry Model 
Refer to Figure 58 below for information about data linkages between the 
substation and the SCADA Telemetry models that reside in the SCADA 
subsystem schema. These linkages identify relationships among the 
SCADA measurement, telemetry, and communication systems.

5.2 Defining an RTU 
RTU records are used to model the data retrieval system, including 
information such as what protocol is used to communicate with the RTU, 
the address to which the RTU responds, and what logging actions should 
occur when alarms are inhibited. 
To define an RTU, complete the following steps: 
1. Click the RTU toolbar button. 
 
The SCADA Telemetry Tree appears in the left pane. 
2. Place the cursor on the Remote Te rminal Unit icon at the top of the 
tree, and click the right mouse button. 
3. Select the Add RTU option. 
The RTU form appears in the right pane. 
4. Define the RTU as follows: 
a. Name the RTU after its parent substation. 
b. Specify the RTU type using the dropdown menu. 
c. Specify the numeric address of the RTU as it is known to the 
scanning system. 
d. Specify the Activate address if th e protocol supports Select Before 
Operate (SBO) requests. (Of the supported RTU protocols, only 
Conitel makes use of the activate address.) 
e. Specify the Freeze address if th e protocol supports Individual 
freeze requests. 
f. Specify the RTU configuration file name, and toggle the Download 
check box ON if the download is required at system startup. 
g. Specify the Area of Responsib ility, Alarm Category, and Alarm 
Location. 
h. Specify other details as required. 
5. Save the record. 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  Remote Terminal Unit (RTU) Definition 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
135
 
 
 
Figure 59: RTU Definition Form 
5.3 Duplicating an RTU Definition 
With the Copy Tree window open next to the SCADA Telemetry window, 
the model builder can duplicate an RTU in the Remote Terminal Units tree 
by dragging the “template” RTU definition into the RTU topology and 
dropping it on the Remote Terminal Units folder. 
5.4 Standard Modeling Rules for All Supported Protocols 
The Communication Front End (CFE) depends upon SCADAMOM to 
supply sufficient information for the CFE to interpret RTU responses 
correctly. For the supported protocols, the rules to achieve this are: 
• That all values returned from t he RTU in response to a single scan 
request must be modeled under a single ADRS. 
• That CARDs be arranged under an ADRS in the same order the RTU 
returns the scan values. 
• That each CARD record represent a single data point type. The 
individual point types are: 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  Remote Terminal Unit (RTU) Definition 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
136
 
− Non-MCD, non-SOE digital inputs. 
− MCD, non-SOE digital inputs. 
− Non-MCD, SOE digital inputs. 
− MCD, SOE digital inputs. 
− 12-bit analog inputs. 
− Accumulators with different numbers of bits in the reading. 12-bit 
accumulators, 24-bit, and 28-bit accumulators are all different point 
types. 
• That each physical point returned by the RTU be modeled. Some 
Conitel RTUs have cards with 24 statuses on them. Rather than have 
the CFE watch for holes in PHYSADDR, the SCADA engineer models 
whatever will appear on the comm line. 
• That each digital input CARD must be full. 
For Conitel, “full” means either: 
− The number of non-MCD CONECT progeny is a multiple of 12. 
− (or) The number of MCD CONECT  progeny is a multiple of 6. 
For Landis & Gyr 12-bit, “full” means that the number of CONECT 
records is a multiple of 8. 
For Vancomm, “full” means either: 
The number of non-MCD CONECT progeny is a multiple of 16. 
(or) The number of MCD CONECT progeny is a multiple of 8. 
For the unit status CARD, the number of non-MCD CONECT progeny 
is a multiple of 8. 
For GETAC, “full” means that the number of CONECT records is a 
multiple of 20. 
For CDC, “full” means that the number of CONECT records is a 
multiple of 16. 
5.5 Protocol-Specific Modeling Rules 
This topic addresses telemetry modeling requirements compelled by the 
communication protocol of the scanned RTU. 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  Remote Terminal Unit (RTU) Definition 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
137
 
5.5.1 Conitel Protocol 
To model an RTU for Conitel protocol, model one RTU record for each 
RTU in the system. Under an RTU record, model one ADRS record for 
each scan group on the RTU. ADDRESS_ADRS contains the scan group 
number. Under an ADRS record, model one CARD record for each 
response type (XDUCER, non-MCD CONECT, MCD CONECT, PULSE, 
RELAY, ANOUT). CARD records must be ordered corresponding to the 
order that the RTU will respond with each point type when that group is 
scanned. Under each CARD, model the number of data values (choose 
one of the following records: XDUCER, CONECT (non-MCD), CONECT 
(MCD), PULSE, RELAY, ANOUT) present in the RTU for that response 
type and group. 
For each analog value retrieved, there must be an XDUCER record. Cards 
that retrieve digital status values are a little more complicated: a CONECT 
record represents one or two bits in a 12-bit data section. The entire 
section is always retrieved so, even if some of the bits are unused, there 
must be a CONECT record for each bit to “pad out” the section. 
In the bit stream from a digital status card, the first bit received is the high 
order bit. This bit is represented by the first CONECT record under a 
CARD. For the two-bit status values, the first bit received is the S bit, the 
second is the X bit. 
For Conitel, the value of ADDRESS_ADRS must be unique for each 
ADRS on an RTU. 
Only two ANOUT records can be defined with one Conitel address 
(modeled as an ADRS record). The first ANOUT will correspond to 
SETPOINT A, and the second ANOUT will correspond to SETPOINT B. If 
only SETPOINT B is to be used, SETPOINT A (the first ANOUT under the 
ADRS) must still be modeled and left as a spare by not providing linkage 
to the SUBSTN hierarchy. 
5.5.2 Landis & Gyr 12-Bit Protocol 
For Landis & Gyr protocol RTUs, model one RTU record for each RTU in 
the system. Under an RTU record, model one ADRS record for each RTU 
request (Analog Force, ADC Reference, Indication Force, SOE Force, 
Accumulator Force). 
If the RTU response is long enough to require a continuation, use multiple 
ADRS records to force multiple requests. Under the ADRS record, model 
one CARD record. Under the CARD record, model the number of points 
returned in the RTU response. There must be an XDUCER record for 
each analog value retrieved. There will also be one CONECT record for 
each point for which data is retrieved. 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  Remote Terminal Unit (RTU) Definition 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
138
 
The CONECT record represents either two or four bits, with no division 
into sections. For a simple status point, there are two bits, the first being 
the status bit (S bit) and the second being the momentary change bit 
(M bit), which can be ignored. 
For three-state status points, there are two bits for the point’s status (S bit 
and X bit), and a momentary change bit for each status bit. One of the 
momentary change bits is used to set the M bit. 
For Landis & Gyr RTUs, all of the CONECT records must be modeled 
under one ADRS record. MCD and non-MCD CONECTs should be 
modeled under separate CARDs, under the same ADRS. 
If an ADRS record models an SOE FORCE request, set the SOE flag on 
the CARD record to TRUE. 
Model one ADRS record under the RTU for SBO requests. Under the SBO 
ADRS, model one CARD. Under the CARD, model two RELAY records for 
each SBO point. The first RELAY models the TRIP relay, and the second 
RELAY models the CLOSE relay. 
5.5.3 Vancomm Protocol 
For Vancomm protocol RTUs, model one RTU record for each RTU in the 
system. Under an RTU record, model one ADRS record for each RTU 
request (Analog Request, Status Dump). Also, model one non-periodic 
ADRS (rate is zero) for the unit status report. Under an ADRS record, 
model one CARD record for each response type (XDUCER, unit-status 
CONECTs, non-MCD CONECT, MCD CONECT, PULSE, RELAY, 
ANOUT). CARD records must be ordered corresponding to the order that 
the RTU will respond with each point type when that group is scanned. 
Under each CARD, model the number of data values (choose one of the 
following records: XDUCER, CONECT (non-MCD), CONECT (MCD), 
PULSE, RELAY, ANOUT) present in the RTU for that response type and 
group. The unit-status ADRS should have one CARD record, which has 8 
children CONECT records. Except for the unit status ADRS, the 
SCADAMOM database must match the RTU’s database. 
For Vancomn RTUs, the unit-status CONECTs must be modeled under 
the first ADRS record, and all other CONECT records must be modeled 
under one ADRS record. MCD and non-MCD CONECTs should be 
modeled under separate CARDs, under the same ADRS. 
5.5.4 GETAC Protocol 
For GETAC protocol RTUs, model one RTU record for each RTU in the 
system. Under an RTU record, model one ADRS record for each RTU 
request (Scan Digital Inputs, Scan Analog Inputs). All CONECT records 
should be modeled under one ADRS, and all XDUCER and PULSE 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  Remote Terminal Unit (RTU) Definition 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
139
 
records should be modeled under one ADRS. The XDUCER and PULSE 
records will be modeled under separate CARD records within the one 
ADRS record. Toggle the MCD bit to TRUE for all CONECT records, since 
the CFE will construct a single report for multiple changes of state 
occurring between scans. 
5.5.5 CDC Protocol 
For CDC protocol RTUs, model one RTU record for each RTU in the 
system. Under an RTU record, model one ADRS record for normal scan 
data (CONECTs and XDUCERs), one for each accumulator scan address 
(PULSEs), one or more for relay controls (RELAYS), and one or more for 
analog output controls (ANOUTs). Model each different type of scan data 
(CONECTs, MCD-CONECTS, and XDUCERs) under a separate CARD 
record. Under each CARD record, model the number of points returned in 
the RTU response. There must be an XDUCER record for each analog 
value retrieved. There will also be one CONECT record for each point for 
which data is retrieved. The CONECT record represents either two or four 
bits, and there is no division into sections. 
For a simple status point, there are two bits, the first being the status bit 
(S bit) and the second being the momentary change bit (M bit), which can 
be ignored. For three-state status points, there are two bits for the point’s 
status (S bit and X bit), and a momentary change bit for each status bit. 
One of the momentary change bits is used to set the M bit. 
For CDC RTUs, all of the CONECT and XDUCER records must be 
modeled under one ADRS record. MCD CONECTs, non-MCD CONECTs, 
and XDUCERs should be modeled under separate CARDs, under the 
same ADRS, and the CONECT cards should precede the XDUCER cards. 
Model one ADRS record under the RTU for accumulator scan data. 
Under the ADRS, model one or more CARD records. Under the CARD 
record, model one PULSE record for each accumulator input. 
Model one or more ADRS records under the RTU for relay control. Under 
the ADRS, model one or more CARD records. Under the CARD record, 
model two RELAY records for each controllable device. The first RELAY 
models the TRIP relay, and the second RELAY models the CLOSE relay. 
Model one or more ADRS records under the RTU for analog output 
control. Under the ADRS, model one or more CARD records. Under the 
CARD record, model one ANOUT record for each analog output device. 
Model one or more ADRS records under the RTU for pulse-width-output 
control. Under the ADRS, model one or more CARD records. Under the 
CARD record, model four RELAY records for each CDC-type PWO card. 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  Remote Terminal Unit (RTU) Definition 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
140
 
The first and third RELAY records model the RAISE control, and the 
second and fourth RELAY records model the LOWER control. 
5.5.6 Protocol Records 
Special protocol requirements are defined using records in the Remote 
Telemetry data structure. The record types are described as follows: 
• PCLRTU Records 
These records allow protocol-specific information to be modeled in a 
generic way for a particular RTU. If the RTU protocol design note 
requires it, insert the required number of PCLRTU records and fill in 
the required IDs and values. 
• PCLADR Records 
These records allow protocol-specific information to be modeled in a 
generic way for a particular ADRS. If the RTU protocol design note 
requires it, insert the required number of PCLADR records and fill in 
the required IDs and values. 
• PCLCRD Records 
These records allow protocol-specific information to be modeled in a 
generic way for a particular CARD. If the RTU protocol design note 
requires it, insert the required number of PCLCRD records and fill in 
the required IDs and values. 
5.6 Defining a Scan Group 
Scan Group (ADRS) records correspond to a single RTU request. Scan 
Groups are often organized by measurement type (analog, status, 
accumulator). However, all the data associated with a Scan Group must 
be scanned at a single rate, since the rate to obtain related data from the 
RTU is entered on the Scan Group record. 
Information modeled may include the number of immediate retries to be 
performed by the Telemetry Front End if communication errors are 
encountered, the number of communication errors to process before 
failing the Scan Group, etc. 
Each Scan Group may also have associated Scan Group protocol records 
as children. 
To define an RTU scan group, complete the following steps: 
1. Expand the RTU folder to expose its Scan Groups folder. 
2. Place the cursor on the Scan Groups  folder, and click the right mouse 
button. 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  Remote Terminal Unit (RTU) Definition 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
141
 
3. Select the A dd ADRS option. 
4. Define the Scan Group as follows: 
a. Specify the name of the Scan Group. 
b. Specify the Data retrieval address and Scan rate. 
c. Specify the Not-In-Service (NIS) state of the Scan Group at system 
startup. 
d. Specify the applic able Scanning method. 
e. Specify the scanning protocol usin g the Scan Group Protocol folder 
that resides in the Scan Group folder with the Card definitions. 
f. Specify other details as required. 
5. Save the record. 
 
 
Figure 60: Scan Group Definition 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  Remote Terminal Unit (RTU) Definition 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
142
 
5.7 Modeling Requirements for CARD Record Structures 
The schema modeler associates Card (CARD) records with one kind of 
RTU measurement record: either a Connect, Transducer, Analog Output, 
Pulse, Relay, Transducer Reference, or Connect Reference card type. 
A physical card that is split between data retrieval addresses will have 
more than one Card record. 
The CARD record has a link to one of the following offspring records: 
• Connect (CONECT) records 
CONECT records identify RTU connections that are wired for incoming 
status (1- or 2-bit) information. This record type models such things as 
the physical address of the measurement in the RTU, whether multiple 
change detection (MCD) information is provided from the RTU, 
whether the Connect record models a four-state measurement, and 
whether the status bit should be inverted. 
The Scanner program has the following wiring and data stream 
requirements: 
− Two-bit status values must be wired to consecutive bits on a card. 
− These status values must appear consecutively in the data stream. 
CONECT records are required for data retrieved from local digital I/O 
modules. In this case, physical hardware at the CARD record level 
does not impose restrictions on the numbers of CONECTs to each 
card. 
The data retrieved from a digital input card is converted for display on 
a POINT record. 
Note: The protocol-specific requirements for this record are as follows: 
− For Landis & Gyr RTUs, all of the CONECTs must be modeled 
under one ADRS record. MCD and non-MCD CONECTs are 
modeled under different CARDs under the same ADRS record. 
− For Conitel RTUs, the CONECT s are modeled under the ADRS, 
which is associated with the scan group for which the status data 
will be returned. However, MCD and non-MCD CONECTs must be 
modeled under different CARDs under the same ADRS record. 
− For Vancomm RTUs, the first CARD of CONECT records must 
contain 8 CONECTs, and it is a child of a non-periodic ADRS. This 
is to account for the unit status report from the RTU. 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  Remote Terminal Unit (RTU) Definition 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
143
 
− For GETAC RTUs, all of the CONECTs must be modeled under 
one ADRS record. MCD and non-MCD CONECTs are modeled 
under different CARDs under the same ADRS record. 
− For CDC RTUs, all of the CONECTs must be modeled under one 
ADRS record. MCD and non-MCD CONECTs are modeled under 
different CARDs under the same ADRS record. 
If the measurement is represented in the power system model, the 
Connect record will be associated with a Substation, Device Type, 
Device, and Point record. 
• Transducer (XDUCER) records 
These records provide information about the connection between 
analog values from an analog input card and the power system 
database, and model non-discrete analog measurements from the 
RTU. 
Insert one XDUCER record for each analog measurement in the 
incoming data stream. XDUCER records correspond to 12 bits of 
analog data. 
XDUCER records are required for analog data retrieved from local 
digital I/O modules. In this case, physical hardware at the CARD 
record level does not impose restrictions on the numbers of XDUCERs 
on each card. 
Data retrieved from a transducer is stored in the XDUCER record and 
converted for display on an ANALOG record in the SUBSTN subtree. 
Information modeled on the XDUCER record determines how to 
convert the raw RTU values to engineering units. Conversion (CONV) 
records can be used to model piece-wise linear conversion, if straight 
linear conversion is not sufficient. 
• Pulse (PULSE) records 
These records provide the connection between pulse accumulator 
values from an accumulator card at the RTU and the power system 
database. The requirements for inserting PULSE records are similar to 
those for XDUCER records. 
Insert one PULSE record for each pulse accumulator measurement. 
PULSE records usually correspond to 12 bits of count data; however, 
the number of bits returned from the RTU for each measurement is 
defined on the CTRTYP record in VALTAB, which allows up to 32 bits 
of data to be modeled for that counter type. 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  Remote Terminal Unit (RTU) Definition 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
144
 
Data retrieved from a pulse accumulator is converted for display on a 
COUNT record in the SUBSTN subtree. 
Information is modeled on the PULSE record identifying how to convert 
the raw value to engineering units. 
• Relay (RELAY) records 
RELAY records provide information about the connection between 
control relays and the power system devices controlled, and model 
controllable relays in the RTU. 
Each RELAY record represents a single relay. The data in the CTRL 
record determines what action the relay takes when activated by a 
control request. 
Protocol and RTU-specific information required for Supervisory Control 
is also modeled on the RELAY record. 
If this measurement is represented in the power system model, the 
RELAY record will be associated with a Substation, Device Type, 
Device, Point, and Control record. 
• Analog Output (ANOUT) records 
These records model analog-output devices available in the RTU, and 
provide information about the connection between analog values from 
a setpoint record in the power system database and an analog control 
output. Insert one ANOUT record for each analog output in the 
address. ANOUT records correspond to 12 bits of analog output data. 
Data contained in the ANOUT record is used to convert desired 
engineering unit data to raw counts for transmission to the RTU. 
Note: Only two ANOUT records may be defined with one Conitel 
address (modeled as an ADRS record). The first ANOUT will 
correspond to SETPOINT A and the second ANOUT will correspond to 
SETPOINT B. If only SETPOINT B is to be used, SETPOINT A (the 
first ANOUT under the ADRS) must still be modeled and left as a spare 
by not providing linkage to the SUBSTN hierarchy. 
If this supervisory control is represented in the power system model, 
this Analog Output record will be associated with a Substation, Device 
Type, Device, Analog, and Setpoint record. Protocol and RTU-specific 
information required for Supervisory Control is modeled on the Analog 
Output record. 
• Transducer Reference (XDUREF) records 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  Remote Terminal Unit (RTU) Definition 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
145
 
These records are used to model ADRS records that represent analog 
group scans. Only Landis & Gyr protocol supports analog group 
scanning. 
For analog group scanning, the analogs on an RTU are scanned in a 
logical order, not in their physical order. The definition of an analog 
group ADRS consists of an ADRS and a CARD, followed by one 
XDUREF record for each analog measurement that is part of the 
logical group. The XDUREF records actually are logical references to 
physical analogs represented by XDUCER records. Data retrieved 
using XDUREF records is stored on XDUCER records. 
The following restrictions must be followed when modeling analog 
group data: 
− If any XDUCER child of an ADRS belongs to a group, then all 
XDUCERs under that ADRS should belong to a group. VERIFY 
issues a warning for every XDUCER found that does not belong to 
a group when other XDUCER(s) under the ADRS are assigned to a 
group. They do not have to belong to the same group. 
− Analog points cannot be scanned both individually by physical 
address and also as a member of a group. If an analog is assigned 
to a group, the XDUCER must be a child of a non-periodic ADRS 
(therefore, it will not be scanned by a physical address, only by the 
logical grouping). 
− All XDUREF children of a l ogical grouping should be modeled 
under one CARD record, and only one CARD record should be 
modeled under the parent ADRS (each analog group should be 
modeled under separate ADRS records). 
− Each group of XDUCER records that have consecutive physical 
addresses and are referenced by XDUREF records should be 
modeled under one CARD record, and only one CARD record 
should be modeled under the parent ADRS. The ADRS record 
should be non-periodic (RATE of zero). 
− The XDUREF records must model t he analogs in the order they are 
returned from the RTU when the analog group is scanned. 
− The parent ADRS of the XDUREF record is used to determine 
whether analog dump or analog exception scanning is to be 
performed, rather than the parent ADRS of the associated 
XDUCER records, since those ADRS records are non-periodic. 
• Connect Reference (CONREF) record s model Connects in a logical 
fashion, in addition to being modeled by a physical address. 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  Remote Terminal Unit (RTU) Definition 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
146
 
The order of the Connect Reference record matches the order in which 
the discrete measurements are returned from the RTU for the parent 
Scan Group.

2. Locate the Scan Group of interest and select the icon to expose the 
tree of CARD folders: 
− Analog Input Card 
− Analog Output Card 
− Connect Reference Card 
− Digital Control Relay Card 
− Pulse Accumulator Card 
− Status Input Card 
− Transducer Reference Card 
3. Place the cursor on the card fol der of interest, and click the right 
mouse button.

Proprietary – See Copyright Page  Remote Terminal Unit (RTU) Definition 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide
