Figure 61: Analog Input CARD Definition 
5.9 SCADA Conversion Records 
SCADA Conversion (CONV) records define conversion processing, which 
may optionally be applied to any Connect or Transducer measurement 
defined in the Remote Telemetry model. 
Conversion records usage follows these rules: 
• When three-state or four-state di screte measurements need to reorder 
or flip. If the bit values received from the RTU for this associated 
measurement must be flipped because the connection in the RTU is 
wired backwards, a CONV record must be inserted. This is also true if 
the bit values do not match the chosen convention of “Open” or 
“Closed” points in the Remote Telemetry model. 
• Linear analog conversion information is kept on the XDUCER record. If 
the Conversion table of the Analog Input Telemetry Point record on the 
Analog Input Card form is blank, then all conversion data needed is in 
the XDUCER record. 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  Remote Terminal Unit (RTU) Definition 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
148
 
• If the ANALOG has a linear conversi on, the slope and intercept fields 
are computed from the raw and engineering values entered on the 
CARD display. For the non-linear conversion, the slope and intercept 
for each segment are calculated by SCADAMDL bulk validation from 
the DATA records. 
• The paired data points r epresented by DATA records are derived from 
the calibration curve for the transducer. The data processing programs 
will linearly interpolate these points to scale raw values to engineering 
units. Each DATA record represents the end point of a segment, and a 
slope and intercept are calculated for each segment. If there is a 
pointer to a non-linear conversion, then at least two DATA records 
must follow the CONV record. 
5.10 Defining Conversion Tables 
Conversion table records (CONV) define data conversion processing, 
which may optionally be applied to any Status Input Telemetry Point or 
Analog Input Telemetry Point measurement defined in the model. 
Conversion records are required for three-state or four-state discrete 
measurements whose telemetered bit values must be reordered or flipped. 
The conversion is usually required because the connection in the RTU is 
wired backwards, or it does not match the chosen convention of “Open” or 
“Closed” Status points in the model. 
Linear analog conversion information is kept on the Analog Input 
Telemetry Point (XDUCER) record. If the Use Conversion Table field of 
the Analog Input Telemetry point is “none”, then all conversion data 
needed is in the XDUCER record. 
If the ANALOG has a linear conversion, the slope and intercept fields are 
computed from the raw and engineering values entered on the Card 
general edit form. For the non-linear conversion, the slope and intercept 
for each segment are calculated by VERIFY from the DATA records. 
The paired data points represented by Data for Conversion (DATA) 
records are derived from the calibration curve for the transducer. The data 
processing programs will linearly interpolate these points to scale raw 
values to engineering units. Each DATA record represents the end point of 
a segment, and a slope and intercept are calculated for each segment. If 
there is a pointer to a non-linear conversion, then at least two DATA 
records must follow the CONV record. 
To define conversion tables, complete the following steps: 
1. Select the Generic SCADA T ables option in the View menu. 
The Reference Tables view displays. 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  Remote Terminal Unit (RTU) Definition 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
149
 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  Remote Terminal Unit (RTU) Definition 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
150
2. Expand the Conversion Tables fo lder to expose its contents. 
a. To add a new conversion table, place the cursor on the Conversion 
Tables folder, click the right mouse button, and select the Add 
CONV option. 
b. To update an existing conversi on table, select its icon. 
3. Define conversion table informa tion in the SCADA Conversions 
reference table. 
 
 
6. SCADA Communications Definition 
Data acquisition in the distributed e-terrascada subsystem is 
accomplished through a combination of software and hardware. This 
consists of e-terrascada applications running on the e-terrascada host 
computer, the telemetry front end (TFE) applications, the embedded 
communications card, the communications front end (CFE) code, and the 
RTU responding to scan requests. 
The host periodically sends scan authorization messages to the TFE so 
that it continues to scan RTUs un-assisted. The TFE continues to buffer 
data until the authorized period expires or a new host assumes the 
primary role. 
The communications front end (CFE) performs three core functions: 
• Translates the RTU communications pr otocol into a standard protocol, 
keeping the host and TFE software standard across any number of 
different RTU protocols. 
• Concentrates a number of communicati ons lines into a single network 
interface to the TFE. 
• Filters RTU responses into report-by-exception reporting. 
6.1 Defining TFEs 
TFE records provide information by modeling the communications system, 
and they represent individual, logical Telemetry Front Ends that may or 
may not be running on a separate computer. 
TFEs prioritize RTU requests. In a dual-redundant configuration, TFEs 
may be configured to continue scanning when the SCADA Host CPU fails 
to the secondary, and to return the data gathered to the new host when it 
becomes primary. 
To define TFEs, complete the following steps: 
1. Click the Communications toolbar button. 
2. Expand the Telemetry Front Ends folder to expose its contents. 
a. To define a new TFE, place the cursor on the Telemetry Front Ends 
folder or an existing TFE, click the right mouse button, and select 
the Add TFE option. 
b. To update an existing TFE, select its icon. 
3. Define TFE information using the Telemetry Front End form. 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  SCADA Communications Definition 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
151
 
 
 
Figure 62: TFE Definition 
6.2 Defining CFEs 
The Communications Front End consists of a single-board 
communications controller that is connected to the TFE using TCP/IP 
network connections. Each board handles from four to sixteen 
communications lines. All RTU-protocol–specific handling is done on 
these controllers. 
CFE records contain the following information: 
• Channels: Channel records model a single Communications Front 
End (CFE) configured on the TFE. Generally, the Channel is 
associated with a single card on a local bus or in a remote chassis. 
Channel information includes identifying the image to be downloaded 
to the CFE when it is initially configured or restarted. 
• Paths: Path records model a single communications line from the CFE 
to one or more physical RTUs. The communications line may be a 
physical link, or a link of any other type, such as microwaves. Path 
information includes identifying the no-reply timeout and interbyte gap 
in milliseconds, the baud rate used to retrieve data, whether the 
communication line uses constant carrier, sequence of events, whether 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  SCADA Communications Definition 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
152
 
more than one communication line is bridged together with this one, 
etc. 
Note: Multiple paths can exist from the CFE(s) to each RTU, 
depending on redundancy requirements. The CFE is controlled by the 
TFE, which is in turn controlled by the host SCADA processes. A 
database with RTU information, communications line information, and 
protocol information is downloaded to the CFE from the TFE at startup 
or when there is a change in the field configuration. 
• RTU Communication Data: RTU Communication Data records 
(RTUCs) associate an RTU with a communications path. The RTU 
name must match an existing (modeled) RTU record. 
To define CFEs, complete the following steps: 
1. Click the Communications toolbar button. 
2. Expand the appropriate Telemetry Front Ends folder to expose its 
contents. 
a. To define a new CFE, place the cursor on an existing TFE or CFE, 
click the right mouse button, and select the Add CHANEL option. 
b. To update an existing CFE, select its icon. 
3. Define CFE information using the Communication Front End form. 
4. If required, define CFE c hannel protocol information. 
a. Expand the appropriate channel icon. 
i. To define CFE channel information, place the cursor on the 
Channel Protocol-Specific Info folder, click the right mouse 
button, and select the Add PCLCHN option. 
ii. To update existing channel information for a CFE, expand the 
Channel Protocol-Specific Info folder and select the appropriate 
icon. 
b. Enter CFE channel information using the Communication Channel 
form. 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  SCADA Communications Definition 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
153
 
 
 
Figure 63: CFE Definition

To define the communications paths between a CFE and one of its RTUs, 
complete these steps: 
1. Expand the Channel icon. 
a. To define a communication path, place the cursor on the 
Communication Paths folder, click the right mouse button, and 
select the Add PATH option. 
b. To update existing channel info rmation for a CFE, expand the 
Communication Paths folder and select the appropriate icon. 
2. Define CFE-RTU communication path information using the 
Communication Path form. 
3. If required, define CFE-RTU comm unication path protocol information. 
a. Expand the appropriate path icon. 
b. To define protocol information fo r a communication path, place the 
cursor on the Path Protocol-Specific Info folder, click the right 
mouse button, and select the Add PCLPTH option. 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  SCADA Communications Definition 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
154
 
c. To update existing protocol information for a communication path, 
expand the Path Protocol-Specific Info folder and select the

CRC/BCH Errors, and Other Communication Errors.

button, and select the Add BUCKET option.

icon.

7. SCADA Calculation Functions 
This chapter provides information about the argument notation of each of 
the calculation functions supported by the e-terrascada subsystem. 
7.1 Calculation Functions 
Calculations are normally triggered by the change of a scanned value that 
is part of a Measurement Group or referenced from a Measurement 
Group. Calculations can also be done on a timed basis. The Measurement 
Group can be associated with a Timer record to link the calculation to that 
timer. 
The standard calculations implemented in the Scanner are listed below. 
The two-letter designation following the name is the subroutine code that 
identifies the function to the Scanner. The next designation is the function 
list name, as initially provided. This is the name that should appear on the 
measurement group records. The name can be changed on the function 
list display. The argument list under the measurement group record is 
given, and pseudo FORTRAN describes the calculation. 
SCADA calculations are described in the following sections. 
7.1.1 General Analog Calculations 
This section lists and describes the general analog calculations. These 
calculations have the argument Analog Input (ANALOG), Accumulator 
Input (COUNT), or SCADA Constant (RCONST).

7.2 Defining SCADA Calculation Functions 
Modeling a SCADA calculation requires the modeler to complete four 
tasks: calculation definition, subroutine definition, calculation validation, 
and subroutine testing. 
This topic addresses calculation definition only. 
To define a SCADA calculation function, complete the following steps: 
1. Select the Generic SCADA T ables option in the View menu. 
The Reference Tables view displays. 
2. Expand the Calculation Functions folder to expose its contents. 
3. Place the cursor on the Calculati on Functions folder, click the right 
mouse button, and select the Add FUNC option. 
4. Enter SCADA calculation function details in the fields. 
 
 
Figure 67: SCADA Calculation Function Definition 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  SCADA Calculation Functions 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
175
 
7.3 Defining SCADA Calculation Argument References 
Reference records are grouped under Measurement Group records for the 
purpose of referencing other measurements for a special calculation or 
special control sequence. Different reference records exist for referencing 
Analog, Point, Count, Constant, Setpoint, and Control records. 
To model calculation argument references for measurement groups 
associated with SCADA devices or other equipment in a substation, 
complete the following steps: 
1. Click the Equipment  toolbar button. 
2. Navigate to the appropriate substation icon, and expand it. 
3. Expand the Measurement Group ic on (in the appropriate device or 
equipment folder) to expose its contents. 
4. Model the calculat ion function arguments. 
a. To model a new SCADA calculation argument record, place the 
cursor on the appropriate folder, click the right mouse button, and 
select one of the following options: Add CNTREF, ALGREF, 
CTLREF, CONREF, SETREF, and PNTREF records to the 
associated Calculation Argument Reference form. 
b. Model the SCADA calculation argument record in the designated 
Reference form. 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  SCADA Calculation Functions 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide

Proprietary – See Copyright Page  SCADA Calculation Functions 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide

7.4 Assigning Calculation Function Arguments 
To assign the arguments in a SCADA function to their input data, 
complete these steps: 
1. Open the equipment folder where the Measurement Group of interest 
resides. 
2. Select the Measurement Group ic on to display the measurement group 
details, and select Measurement Type Calculation from the dropdown 
menu. 
3. Select a function from t he Function Type dropdown menu. 
Notice that the list of required arguments appears in the Argument 
Assignment table at the bottom of the form. For example, the 
arguments for the MVA calculation function appear as ARG1, ARG2, 
and ARG3 in the table. 
4. Select the table row for the ARG record of interest. 
5. Open the SCADA device tree in the Argument Assignment window at 
the top of the form. 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  SCADA Calculation Functions 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
178
 
6. Locate the data point under the measured device, and select the data 
point that the highlighted ARG record in the Argument Assignment 
table must be linked to. 
Note: This will activate the Assign button. 
7. Click the Assign button. 
 
 
Figure 70: Argument Assignment 
To modify the argument assignment, complete these steps: 
1. Highlight the measur ed device data point in the Argument Assignment 
window. 
2. Select the new target ARG record  in the Argument Assignment table. 
Notice that the Assign button above the argument name table is 
activated. 
3. Click the Assign button. 
4. Notice that the ARG record ident ifier moves to the new data point in 
the Argument Assignment window. 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  SCADA Calculation Functions 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
179
 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  SCADA Calculation Functions 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
180
5. Click the Save button in the toolbar to  save this change, or click Cancel 
to cancel. 
 
 
8. SCADA Mapping 
This chapter describes the general principles, rules, and tables for 
mapping Generation and Network components to Measurement 
Structures assigned to SCADA devices. 
SCADAMOM has various measurement types that can potentially be 
mapped to components in NETMOM (or GENMOM). The transfer of 
measurements from SCADAMOM to NETMOM (or SCADAMOM to 
GENMOM) is done through measurement mapping to corresponding 
database records. That is, when a record in one database corresponds to 
a record in another database, a map can be established. 
8.1 Shared Components 
Operating divisions, their parent Operating Areas, and any power 
equipment data shared among the subsystem models are represented in 
the e-terramodeler PowerModel schema. Some of the cross-model 
relationships encapsulated in the PowerModel are illustrated in the figure 
below:

8.2 Mapping Rules for the e-terrageneration Subsystem 
This section discusses component mapping rules specific to the 
e-terrageneration subsystem. 
8.2.1 SCADAMOM Map Names 
In the SCADAMOM hierarchy, DEVTYPs records are used to group 
devices of the same type within each station. For example, the 
SCADAMDL user might define a DEVTYP record for tie corridors, another 
for tie-lines, and another for units in a station. 
MAP_TYPNAMS, which are hard-coded in the mapping software, define 
the type of component in GENMOM to which corresponding DEVTYPs in 
SCADAMOM can be mapped. AREVA T&D provides the list of 
MAP_TYPNAMs and their corresponding GENMOM record types. 
ID_TYPNAMs, on the other hand, are defined by SCADAMDL users. In a 
SCADA Point Names table, the SCADAMDL user inserts TYPNAM 
 
records, gives them IDs, and then enters the MAP_TYPNAM they 
correspond to. 
ID_TYPNAMs define the valid DEVTYPs (ID_DEVTYP) that can be 
entered on SCADAMDL’s topology display, SUBTOP. For a map to be 
established, the ID_DEVTYP entered on the SUBTOP display must 
correspond to an ID_TYPNAM entered on the VALTAB display. The 
mapping software only recognizes an ID_DEVTYP that has been defined 
on VALTAB (the SCADAMOM database does not validate if invalid 
ID_DEVTYPs have been used). 
More than one ID_TYPNAM can be defined for a MAP_TYPNAM. For 
example, for MAP_TYPNAM = UN, you could define the following 
ID_TYPNAMs: 
− ID_TYPNAM = UN (for units). 
− ID_TYPNAM = UNIT (for  another type of unit). 
− ID_TYPNAM = UN1 (for another type of unit). 
Using the same example, to establish a map with a UNIT component in 
GENMODEL, it would have to belong to one of the device types above 
(UN, UNIT, or UN1). In this way, your utility can define a set of IDs that are 
typically used at your installation and are to be used in the specification of 
IDs for DEVTYPs, while still complying with the hard-coded mapping 
requirements of the software. 
Map names have been hard-coded for other measurement types as well: 
MAP_PNTNAM and MAP_ALGNAM. These define the valid ID_POINTs 
and ID_ANALOGs that can be entered on the SCADAMDL Topology 
display, SUBTOP. 
Again, in the SCADA Point Names table, the SCADAMDL user inserts 
records for these map names, enters their IDs (ID_PNTNAM and 
ID_ALGNAM), and enters the map name they correspond to. 
8.2.2 PLC Control Mapping 
Each controllable PLC must be mapped to records in SCADAMOM to 
allow control of the PLC by AGC. Depending on how your system is set 
up, each controllable PLC either maps to two CTRL records, for sending 
raise and lower pulses, or to one SETPNT record for sending setpoints. 
Figure 72 shows which matches must occur between the fields in the 
SCADAMOM mappable records and the various fields in the GENMOM 
mappable record. 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  SCADA Mapping 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
183
 
 
 
Figure 72: Mapping PLC Controls 
For either CTRL or SETPNT records, the ID_SUBSTN must match the 
ID_PL, the ID_DEVTYP must map to “PLC”, and the ID_DEVICE must 
match the ID_PLC. 
Each setpoint PLC may have CTRL records with IDs that map to 
“RESTORE” and “FAIL” if it is going to send controls to restore or fail the 
D/A converter based on the value of the setpoint feedback signal. Those 
PLCs receiving raise/lower pulses must have CTRL records with IDs that 
map to “GRAISE” and “GLOWER”. 
PLCs that are on setpoint control must map to SETPNT records with IDs 
that map to “AGC”. 
8.2.3 JOU Control and Measurement Mapping 
Jointly Owned Units must be mapped to records in SCADAMOM to allow 
the transfer of information between the control center of the operating 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  SCADA Mapping 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
184
 
owner and the control centers of all of the share owners of the JOU. This 
transfer takes place in both directions, as the share owners send the 
basepoint requests for their shares to the operating owner and the 
operating owner sends each share’s MW output, limits, and operational 
status to each share owner. 
Since only the control center OPA is modeled in SCADAMDL, all of the 
ANALOGs and POINTs to be used for data transfer for the JOU PLCs and 
UNITs are included under the device matching the JOU representation 
used in the control center OPA. If the system is to be used in 
e-terrasimulator, then, for each internally operated JOU share modeled in 
the operating owner’s area, there is an externally operated JOU modeled 
in the share owner’s area. These matching units and PLCs map to the 
same devices in SCADA, and the data transfer takes place by one side 
putting an ANALOG or POINT value in and the other side taking it out. 
If the control center OPA has a share of a JOU operated by another OPA, 
then the SCADAMDL will have a unit and a PLC device that map to the 
externally operated JOU unit and PLC entered in the control center OPA 
in GENMODEL. These devices need ANALOGs (at least one “MW” 
analog is required) and POINTs for all of the data that are brought in for 
that unit and PLC. This should be followed by, for the unit, an ANALOG 
(possibly with a SETPNT) to be used to send out the basepoint request to 
the operating owner. The PLC device is only needed if a PLC AUTO/MAN 
status is being telemetered from the operating owner of the JOU. If the 
control center OPA operates a JOU, the SCADAMDL will (in addition to 
the devices for the physical unit and PLC) have a PLC device that maps to 
the non-physical JOU PLC as well as unit devices that map to each of the 
non-physical internally operated JOUs for the share owners. The unit 
devices need a required ANALOG for the basepoint request measurement 
to be telemetered in, followed by ANALOGs (possibly with SETPNTs) and 
POINTs (possibly with CTRLs) for all of the data to be sent back to the 
share owners. The PLC device is only needed in this case if the PLC 
AUTO/MAN status is being sent out to the share owners. 
JOU control data output can take place using one of two options. The 
SCUSER option, which uses the SCADA API (SCAPI) to deposit 
information into ANALOG and POINT records, is always used in 
e-terrasimulator and can optionally be used in an EMS. The CLUSER 
option, which also uses the SCADA API (SCAPI) to send controls to the 
RTU, requires mapping to SETPNT and CTRL records. 
Figure 73 shows the matches that must occur between SCADAMOM and 
GENMOM for the non-physical PLC and the non-physical units associated 
with an internally operated JOU. 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  SCADA Mapping 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
185
 
 
 
Figure 73: Mapping Internally Operated JOU Controls 
The ID_SUBSTN must match the ID_PL, the ID_DEVTYP must map to 
“PLC” for the PLC and to “UN” for the units, and the ID_DEVICE must 
match the ID_PLC for the PLC and the ID_UNIT for the units. 
If the AUTO/MAN status of the PLC is to be sent out, then the PLC must 
map to an “AM” POINT record for the SCUSER option and/or both “AUTO” 
and “MAN” CTRL records for the CLUSER option. For the unit controls, 
only the units representing shares owned by OPAs other than the EMS 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  SCADA Mapping 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
186
 
OPA have to be mapped to SCADAMOM. Each of these units must have 
an MW output map, which would be an “MW” analog for SCUSER and/or 
a “JUMW” SETPT for CLUSER. The other controls along with the PLC 
AUTO/MAN status are optional for operation. 
For the SCUSER option, the ID_ANALOG must map to “MWH” (MW high 
limit) or “MWL” (MW low limit), and the ID_POINT must map to “ON” 
(on/off), “MWH” (at high limit), or “MWL” (at low limit). 
For the CLUSER option, the ID_SETPNT must map to “JUHI” (MW high 
limit) or “JULO” (MW low limit), and the ID_CTRL must map to “JON” and 
“JOFF” (on/off), “JAH” and “JNAH” (at/not at high limit), or “JAL” and 
“JNAL” (at/not at low limit). 
Figure 74 shows the matches that must occur between SCADAMOM and 
GENMOM for the non-physical unit associated with an externally operated 
JOU. 
 
 
Figure 74: Mapping Externally Operated JOU Controls 
The ID_SUBSTN must match the ID_PL, the ID_DEVTYP must map to 
“UN”, and the ID_DEVICE must match ID_UNIT. For proper operation, a 
way of sending out the MW request to the operator of the JOU is needed. 
If the SCUSER option is used, then the unit must be mapped to a “REQ” 
ANALOG; and/or if the CLUSER option is to be used, then the unit must 
be mapped to a “JREQ” SETPNT record. 
The PNTNAM records for the POINTS in SCADA have to be defined such 
that the PLC AUTO maps to “CLOSED”, the PLC MAN maps to “OPEN”, 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  SCADA Mapping 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
187
 
the unit OFF, AT HI and AT LO map to “OPEN”, and the unit ON, Not AT 
HI and Not AT LO map to “CLOSED”. 
8.2.4 AREA Measurement Mapping 
Area analog measurements, such as area frequency, are specified using 
OPA templates. 
The ID_DEVTYP of the template must map to either “BUS” or “ND”, and 
the ID_ANALOG must map to “HZ”, “DHZ”, “TE”, “XMW”, “DMW”, “GHZ”, 
“GDHZ”, “GTE”, “GXMW”, or “GDMW”. The IDs beginning with “G” can be 
used to indicate generation-subsystem–specific measurements. 
As a minimum, the “HZ” or “DHZ” measurement must be provided for the 
EMS OPA. 
If you want to receive area MW measurements from external operating 
areas, define a template for the desired external MW measurements, then 
use the Generation Telemetry view to assign the AGC-SCADA composite 
keys. 
If you want to exclude non-conforming load or generators from detection 
of NERC disturbances, you must define a template for this and make the 
supporting assignments. 
Note: If there are multiple loads or generators to be excluded during 
NERC disturbance detection, the ANALOG record in SCADAMOM must 
receive its value from a summation function. The ID_SUBSTN, 
ID_DEVICE, and ID_DEVTYP names in SCADAMDL map to GENMODEL 
as described above. The ID_ANALOG for the MW to exclude must map to 
“DMW”. 
8.2.5 PLC Analog and Status Measurements Mapping 
PLC analog measurement records in GENMOM are specified by defining 
analog and status templates for Plant Controllers. The ID_SUBSTN must 
match the ID_PL, the ID_DEVTYP must map to “PLC”, and the 
ID_DEVICE must match the ID_PLC. 
The ID_ANALOG must map to “SPF” for setpoint feedback signals 
(“GSPF” for generation-subsystem–specific signals, if desired), and the 
ID_POINT must map to “AM” for auto/manual status (“GAM” for 
generation-subsystem–specific status, if desired). 
The PNTNAM record for the auto/manual point in SCADA has to be set up 
so that AUTO maps to “CLOSED” and MAN maps to “OPEN”. 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  SCADA Mapping 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
188
 
8.2.6 PL Measurement Mapping 
Analog measurements for plants are specified by defining plant-specific 
templates. 
For each measurement, the ID_DEVTYP must map to “PL”, and the 
ID_ANALOG must map to “PMHW” (plant headwater elevation), “PMTW” 
(plant tailwater elevation), “PMSP” (plant spillway discharge), or “PMTM” 
(water temperature). 
8.2.7 UNIT Analog and Status Measurements Mapping 
UNIT analog measurement records in GENMOM are specified by defining 
unit-specific templates. 
In both cases, the ID_SUBSTN must match the ID_PL, the ID_DEVTYP 
must map to “UN”, and the ID_DEVICE must match the ID_UNIT. 
The ID_ANALOG must map to “HMW” (MW high limit), “LMW” (MW low 
limit), “MW” (current generation), “MXN” (current MVAR), “RR” (response 
rate), “UFR” (unit frequency), “HFZN” (forbidden zone high limit), or 
“LFZN” (forbidden zone low limit). The names beginning with “G” can be 
used to indicate generation-specific measurements. The modeler may not 
need to capture all of the analogs in your system; the values telemetered 
are probably enough. At a minimum, the “MW” measurement must be 
telemetered for all controllable units. 
To process the value for a JOU MW request from a JOU share owner, the 
ID_ANALOG must map to “REQ” for each non-physical unit not belonging 
to the operating OPA in an internally operated JOU. 
The ID_POINT for status measurements must map to “ON” (on/off), 
“HMW” (at MW high limit), “LMW” (at MW low limit), “SLP” (in sliding 
pressure mode), or “AMU” (auto/manual status of the unit). The names 
beginning with “G” can be used to indicate generation-specific 
measurements. 
The PNTNAM records for these POINTs in SCADA have to be set up such 
that AUTO, OFF, AT HI, SLP, and AT LO map to “OPEN” and MAN, ON, 
NOT AT HI, NOT SLP, and NOT AT LO map to “CLOSED”. 
8.2.8 TIE Measurement Mapping 
Analog and status measurements for TIE corridors are specified by 
defining tie corridor-specific templates and mapping the measurements to 
ANALOG and POINT records, respectively, in SCADAMOM. 
The ID_SUBSTN must match the plant, the ID_DEVTYP must map to 
“TIE”, the ID_DEVICE must match the e-terrageneration component, and 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  SCADA Mapping 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
189
 
the ID_ANALOG must map to “MW” for an MW flow measurement. 
“GMW” can be used for generation-subsystem–specific measurements. 
Count measurements for TIE corridors are specified by defining tie-line 
templates and mapping the measurements to COUNT records in 
SCADAMOM. 
The ID_SUBSTN must match the line plant, the ID_DEVTYP must map to 
“TIE”, the ID_DEVICE must match the e-terrageneration component, and 
the ID_COUNT must map to “MWH” for an MWH measurement. “GMWH” 
can be used for generation-subsystem–specific measurements. 
8.2.9 TYLN Measurement Mapping 
Analog measurements for tie-lines (TYLNs) are specified by defining 
templates and mapping the measurements to ANALOG records in 
SCADAMOM. 
Because a measurement can be associated with either end of a tie-line, 
the node where the flow measurement is taken is included in the tie-line’s 
mappable record. In SCADAMOM, the ID_SUBSTN must match 
SUBSTN_TYLN, the ID_DEVICE must match ID_TYLN, and the 
ITPND_ANALOG must match NODE_TYLN. 
Count measurements for tie-lines (TYLNs) are specified by defining tie-
line count templates and mapping the measurements to COUNT records 
in SCADAMOM. 
Whenever a tie-line record maps to a transformer installed as a tie-line, 
the transformer’s ZBR2 (zero impedance branch) record must be part of 
the mappable record. Again, the node where the flow measurement is 
taken is also included in the tie-line’s mappable record. 
For each analog measurement, the ID_DEVTYP must map to “LN”, and 
the ID_ANALOG must map to “MW” for an MW flow measurement (or 
“GMW” for a generation-specific MW flow measurement, if desired). For 
each count measurement, the ID_DEVTYP must map to “LN”, and the 
ID_COUNT must map to “MWH” (or “GMWH” for a generation-specific 
MWH measurement, if desired). 
8.2.10 AREA Load Mapping 
The e-terrageneration subsystem delivers filtered load values to the 
SCADA database every AGC cycle. 
To establish the OPA-to-SCADA Filtered Load Analog mapping, the 
following conditions must be satisfied: 
• SUBSTN_OPA must match ID_SUBSTN. 
• DEVICE_OPA must match ID_DEVICE. 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  SCADA Mapping 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
190
 
• ID_DEVTYP must match either “BUS” or “ND”. 
• ID_ANALOG must be “FLD” (filtered load). 
To set up an analog in SCADA to hold an RTGEN filtered load value from 
an operating area, the modeler assigns the SUBSTN and DEVICE values 
for the OPA to a measurement template. Validation software assumes that 
no mapping is defined if these fields are left blank, so that no errors would 
be generated during validation. In SCADAMDL, insert an ANALOG, so 
that the associated SUBSTN, DEVTYP, and DEVICE map to GENMODEL 
as described above, and enter ID_ANALOG as “FLD”. 
8.3 Mapping Rules for the Network Subsystem 
This section discusses component mapping rules specific to the Network 
subsystem. 
The measurement map between SCADAMOM and NETMOM databases 
is used to: 
• Transfer measurements from SC ADAMOM to NETMOM, for use by 
the RTNET application as input to state estimation. 
• Transfer simulated measuremen t values from NETMOM to 
SCADAMOM in the e-terrasimulator environment. 
The mapping software follows a fixed set of rules and naming conventions 
that determine which correspondences can be made, or “what maps to 
what”. These rules, and most of the names, are “hard-coded” in the 
mapping software. 
This section explains the rules and conventions the modeler must observe 
when building measurement maps between NETMOM and SCADAMOM. 
Depending on the mapping required at your EMS site, the modeler may or 
may not need to observe all the rules and conventions discussed here. 
8.3.1 Mapping Breakers and Switches 
NETMOM’s CB records, which model circuit breakers and switches in the 
network model, are mapped to the SCADA model when the following 
conditions are met: 
• The breakers were modeled in the same substation as the SCADA 
ANALOG or POINT records that you want them to map to (the 
NETMOM ST record has the same ID as the SCADAMOM SUBSTN 
record). 
• The ModelerUI Reference Table Equipment Type map name is not 
used in breaker and switch mapping. 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  SCADA Mapping 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
191
 
• The NETMOM CBTYP record has the same ID as the SCADAMOM 
DEVTYP record. 
• The NETMOM CB record has the same ID as the SCADAMOM 
DEVICE record. 
• The ID of the measured node specified on the SCADA ANALOG 
record matches the ID of one of the nodes of this breaker/switch in the 
network model (ANALOG measurements only; measurement node ID 
is not used to map statuses). 
• The corresponding ModelerUI Refe rence Table SCADA Point map 
name is: 
− “STTS” for open/closed status. (Var iants: “RSTS” indicates used by 
RTNET only, “DSTS” indicates used by e-terrasimulator only.) 
− “A/M” for auto/manual status. 
− “L/R” for local/remote status. 
• The corresponding ModelerUI Refe rence Table SCADA Analog map 
name is: 
− “MW” for MW measurement. (Variants: “RMW” indicates used by 
RTNET only, “DMW” indicates used by e-terrasimulator only.) 
− “MX” for MVAR measurement. (Var iants: “RMX” indicates used by 
RTNET only, “DMX” indicates used by e-terrasimulator only.) 
− “3AM” for 3-phase amp measurements. 
− “1AM” for 1-phase amp measurements. 
− “MVA” for MVA measurements. 
− “PF” for power fa ctor measurements. 
− Voltage measurements are a spec ial case, and are discussed in 
their own section. 
8.3.2 Mapping Shunt Devices (Units, SVS, Loads, Capacitors, and 
Reactors) 
UN, SVS, LD, and CP records model generating units, static Var systems, 
loads, and capacitors/reactors in the network model, respectively. They 
are mapped to the SCADA model when the following conditions are met: 
• The UN, SVS, LD, or CP is model ed in the same substation as the 
SCADA ANALOG or POINT records that you want it to map to (the 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  SCADA Mapping 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
192
 
NETMOM ST record has the same ID as the SCADAMOM SUBSTN 
record). 
• The corresponding ModelerUI Refer ence Table Equipment Type map 
name is: 
− “UN” if the measured equipm ent type is a generating unit. 
− “SVS” if the meas ured equipment type is a static Var system. 
− “CP” if the measured equipment ty pe is a capacitor or reactor. 
− “LN” or “XF” if the measured equipment type is a load 
(measurement is assumed to be associated with a transmission line 
or transformer). 
• The NETMOM UN, SVS, LD, or CP record has the same ID as the 
SCADAMOM DEVICE record. 
• The ID of the measurement node is not used for mapping shunt 
devices. 
• The corresponding ModelerUI Refe rence Table SCADA Point map 
name is “AVR” for auto/manual regulation status measurement. (Not 
applicable to loads.) (Variants: “RAVR” indicates used by RTNET only, 
“DAVR” indicates used by e-terrasimulator only.) 
• The corresponding ModelerUI Refe rence Table SCADA Analog map 
name is: 
− “MW” for MW measurement. (Unit variants: “RMW” indicates used 
by RTNET only, “DMW” indicates used by e-terrasimulator only.) 
(Load variants: “CW” equivalent to “MW”, “RMW” or “RCW” 
indicates used by RTNET only, “DMW” or “DCW” indicates used by 
e-terrasimulator only.) 
− “MX” for MVAR measurement. (Unit variants: “RMX” indicates used 
by RTNET only, “DMX” indicates used by e-terrasimulator only.) 
(Load and SVS variants: “CX” equivalent to “MX”, “RMX” or “RCX” 
indicates used by RTNET only, “DMX” or “DCX” indicates used by 
e-terrasimulator only.) 
− “3AM” for 3-phase amp measurements. 
− “1AM” for 1-phase amp measurements. 
− “MVA” for MVA measurements. 
− “PF” for power fa ctor measurements. 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  SCADA Mapping 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
193
 
− Voltage measurements are a spec ial case, and are discussed in 
their own section. 
8.3.3 Mapping Branches (Transformers, Lines, and Zero-Impedance 
Branches) 
XF, LN, and ZBR records model transformers, transmission lines, and 
zero-impedance branches in the network model, respectively. They are 
mapped to the SCADA model when the following conditions are met: 
• The XF, LN, or ZBR is modeled in the same substation as the SCADA 
ANALOG or POINT records that you want it to map to (the NETMOM 
ST record has the same ID as the SCADAMOM SUBSTN record). 
• The corresponding ModelerUI Refer ence Table Equipment Type map 
name is: 
− “XF” if the measured equipm ent type is a transformer or 
zero-impedance branch. 
− “LN” if the measur ed equipment type is a transmission line or 
zero-impedance branch. 
• The NETMOM XF2, LN2, or ZBR2 (branch end) record has the same 
ID as the SCADAMOM DEVICE record. 
• The ID of the measured node specified on the SCADA ANALOG 
record matches the ID of the node in the network model (ANALOG 
measurements only). The user ensures that the node names match. 
• The corresponding ModelerUI Refe rence Table SCADA Point map 
name is (XF only): 
− “AWR” for phase-shifting tap auto/manual regulation status 
measurement. (Variants: “RAWR” indicates used by RTNET only, 
“DAWR” indicates used by e-terrasimulator only.) 
−  “AVR” for voltage tap auto/manual regulation status measurement. 
(Variants: “RAVR” indicates used by RTNET only, “DAVR” indicates 
used by e-terrasimulator only.) 
• The corresponding ModelerUI Refe rence Table SCADA Analog map 
name is: 
− “MW” or “CW” for MW measurement. (Variants: “RMW” or “RCW” 
indicates used by RTNET only, “DMW” or “DCW” indicates used by 
e-terrasimulator only.) 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  SCADA Mapping 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
194
 
− “MX” or “CX” for MVAR measurement. (Variants: “RMX” or “RCX” 
indicates used by RTNET only, “DMX” or “DCX” indicates used by 
e-terrasimulator only.) 
− “3AM” for 3-phase amp measurements. 
− “1AM” for 1-phase amp measurements. 
− “MVA” for MVA measurements. 
− “PF” for power fa ctor measurements. 
− “PT” for phase tap measurement s (XF only). (Variants: “RPT” 
indicates used by RTNET only, “DPT” indicates used by 
e-terrasimulator only.) 
− “VT” or “LTC” for voltage tap measurements (XF only). (Variants: 
“RVT” or “RLTC” indicates used by RTNET only, “DVT” or “DLTC” 
indicates used by e-terrasimulator only.) 
− Voltage measurements are a spec ial case, and are discussed in 
their own section. 
8.3.4 Mapping Node/Bus Voltage Measurements 
ND records, which model nodes in the network AC model, are mapped to 
the SCADA model when the following conditions are met: 
• The nodes were modeled in the same substation as the SCADA 
analogs that you want them to map to (the NETMOM ST record has 
the same ID as the SCADAMOM SUBSTN record). 
• The corresponding ModelerUI Refer ence Table Equipment Type map 
name is: 
− “ND” or “BUS” if the measured equipment type is a node or bus. 
− “UN” if the measured equipment type is a unit. 
− “CP” if the measured equipment ty pe is a capacitor or reactor. 
− “LN” if the measured equipment  type is a transmission line. 
− “XF” if the measured equipm ent type is a transformer. 
− “CB” if the measured equipment type is a circuit breaker or switch. 
• The ID of the SCADAMOM DEVICE  record is not used for voltage 
measurement mapping. 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  SCADA Mapping 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
195
 
• The ID of the measured node specified on the SCADA ANALOG 
record matches the ID of the node in the network model. The user 
ensures that the node names match. 
• The corresponding ModelerUI Refe rence Table SCADA Analog map 
name is “KV” for voltage measurements. (Variants: “RKV” indicates 
used by RTNET only, “DKV” indicates used by e-terrasimulator only.) 
8.3.5 Mapping Node/Bus Frequency Measurements (e-terrasimulator 
Only) 
ND records, which model nodes in the network AC model, are mapped to 
the SCADA model when the following conditions are met: 
• The nodes were modeled in the same substation as the SCADA 
analogs that you want them to map to (the NETMOM ST record has 
the same ID as the SCADAMOM SUBSTN record). 
• The corresponding ModelerUI Refer ence Table Equipment Type map 
name is “ND” or “BUS”. 
• The ID of the SCADAMOM DEVICE  record is not used for frequency 
measurement mapping. 
• The ID of the measured node specified on the SCADA ANALOG 
record matches the ID of the node in the network model. The user 
ensures that the node names match. 
• The corresponding ModelerUI Refe rence Table SCADA Analog map 
name is: 
− “DHZ” or “DHZ2” for delta frequency measurements. 
− “HZ” or “HZ2” for frequency measurements. 
− “TE” or “TE2” for time error measurements. 
8.3.6 Mapping DC Converters 
DCCNV records model DC converters (valve groups) in the network 
model. They are mapped to the SCADA model when the following 
conditions are met: 
• The DCCNV is modeled in the same substation as the SCADA 
ANALOG or POINT records that you want it to map to (the NETMOM 
ST record has the same ID as the SCADAMOM SUBSTN record). 
• The corresponding ModelerUI Refer ence Table Equipment Type map 
name is “DCVG”. 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  SCADA Mapping 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
196
 
• The NETMOM DCCNV record has the same ID as the SCADAMOM 
DEVICE record. 
• The ID of the measurement node is not used for mapping. 
• The corresponding ModelerUI Refe rence Table SCADA Point map 
name is: 
−  “BLK” for blocked status measurement. 
− “ISO” for isolated status measurement. 
− “BYP” for bypassed status measurement. 
• The corresponding ModelerUI Refe rence Table SCADA Analog map 
name is: 
− “MW” for AC MW measurement. 
− “MX” for AC MVAR measurement. 
− “3AM” for AC 3-phase amp measurements. 
− “1AM” for AC 1-phase amp measurements. 
− “MVA” for AC MVA measurements. 
− “DCV” for DC volt age measurements. 
8.3.7 Mapping DC Poles 
POLE records model DC poles in the network model, and each pole 
consists of one or more DC converters (DCCNV records). POLE records 
are mapped to the SCADA model when the following conditions are met: 
• The measurements for the DC pol e are stored under a substation 
specified in ModelerUI as the measurement substation for the pole or 
(default) under the substation that contains the corresponding DCCNV 
records assigned to the pole. 
• The corresponding ModelerUI Refer ence Table Equipment Type map 
name is “DCPO”. 
• The NETMOM POLE record has the same ID as the SCADAMOM 
DEVICE record. 
• The ID of the measurement node is not used for mapping. 
• The corresponding ModelerUI Refe rence Table SCADA Analog map 
name is: 
− “DCV” for DC voltage measurement. 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  SCADA Mapping 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide

Proprietary – See Copyright Page  SCADA Mapping 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
198
− “DCI” for DC amp measurement. 
− “DCW” for DC power measurement. 
8.3.8 Mapping Synchronizing Data 
e-terrasimulator synchronizing data are mapped to SCADA on a 
substation basis. They are mapped to the SCADA model when the 
following conditions are met: 
• The substation containing the synchr onizing data must be modeled in 
both SCADA and the network model (the NETMOM ST record has the 
same ID as the SCADAMOM SUBSTN record). 
• The corresponding ModelerUI Refe rence Table Equipment Type map 
name is “SYNC”. 
• The ID of the SCADAMOM D EVICE record is not used for 
synchronization data mapping. 
• The ID of the measurement node is not used for mapping. 
• The corresponding ModelerUI Refe rence Table SCADA Point map 
name is “SISI”. 
• The corresponding ModelerUI Refe rence Table SCADA Analog map 
name is: 
− “SIKV” for “I” node CB synchronization voltage measurement. 
− “SRKV” for “Z” node CB synchronization voltage measurement. 
− “SIHZ” for “I” node CB synchronization frequency measurement. 
− “SRHZ” for “Z” node CB synchronization frequency measurement. 
− “SANG” for CB synchronization phase angle measurement. 
 
 
9. e-terratransmission Modeling Tasks 
This chapter provides information about the specifications that determine 
the operating limits of core transmission equipment and determine the 
operating parameters of a company’s transmission network security 
analysis system. 
9.1 Building Operating Schedules for Network Entities 
Schedules allow other static elements of the network topology to change 
with time. Network entities such as loads, generating units, transformers, 
circuit breakers, and capacitor banks are constructed to change their 
operating characteristics over time. 
Network applications, including RTNET, PWRFLOW, Contingency 
Analysis, Optimal Powerflow, and Security Enhancement, use schedules 
to drive the model in order to evaluate and analyze the security and 
efficiency of the power grid. 
Records that make up the Network Schedules data structure: 
• SKEDTY (Schedule Type) records distinguish between types of 
schedules: load area forecast schedules (LDAREA), forecast breaker 
schedules (CBFCST), and regulation schedules (REGSK). 
• TYMSET (Timeset Definition) reco rds represent the time models that 
are built underneath them. Each record has a name, as well as a field 
specifying the type of model represented: either a weekly cycle 
CYCLE) or a linear time trend (TREND). 
CYCLE TYMSET schedules repeat each week; that is, 
CYCLETYMSET time models contain a set of time points (times when 
component values change) that repeat week after week, except for 
defined abnormal dates (ABDATEs) that interrupt the weekly cycle. 
TREND TYMSET schedules are linear time models made up of trend 
points (TRND records), which are selected dates arranged in calendar 
order. This type of TYMSET is used in the modeling of linear functions 
over long time periods. 
• WKSEG (Week Segment) records ar e children of CYCLE TYMSETs. 
They break the week up into groups of consistent daily schedules. 
WKSEGs are used to specify when a new daily schedule becomes 
effective. You add another WKSEG to a TYMSET only when the times 
at which you want the component’s value to change are different from 
those of the previous day. 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  e-terratransmission Modeling Tasks 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
199
 
 
 
Figure 75: WKSEG Definition 
Each WKSEG record contains the day of the week, the time of day the 
WKSEG becomes effective, and the name of the DAYSEG that is used 
in the WKSEG. 
WKSEGs must be in chronological order, from the earliest day and 
time to the latest. The convention is that the week begins on Sunday at 
00:00. The first WKSEG record under a TYMSET may refer to a later 
day and time, such as Monday at 00:00, but if a Sunday WKSEG 
record is defined later in the sequence, it is ignored. Validation warns 
you of non-chronological entries. 
• DAYSEG (Day Segment) records define a “day’s schedule”. Each 
record contains a name for the DAYSEG (which must be unique within 
the TYMSET) and a set of sequential time points (TYMPTs) specifying 
when the values (defined on CBFORSK, LDFORSK, REGSK, or 
LDVARSK) are scheduled to change. 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  e-terratransmission Modeling Tasks 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
200
 
 
 
Figure 76: DAYSEG Definition 
A “day’s schedule” refers to a time period from 00:00 hours to 
23:59 hours. It is a fixed day, unrelated to when a WKSEG becomes 
effective. 
The daily schedule defined by the DAYSEG repeats each day until the 
WKSEG that called it is superseded by a new WKSEG. 
DAYSEG records belong to TYMSET records rather than to WKSEG 
records. This is important when defining abnormal dates, because it 
enables a TYMSET to contain DAYSEGs that are not called by a 
WKSEG. Only the TYMSETs that contain the name of the DAYSEG 
called by an ABDATE are affected by the ABDATE. This flexibility lets 
you control which TYMSETs are affected by the ABDATE. For 
example, if the ABDATE FOURTHOFJULY called a DAYSEG named 
“JULY4”, then only those TYMSETs with a DAYSEG called “JULY4” 
would be affected by the ABDATE. 
The requirement that DAYSEG names need only be unique within a 
particular TYMSET lets you set up a standard naming convention for 
the DAYSEGs. For example, you might have a DAYSEG named 
“FRIDAY” because it begins on Friday in each of 10 different 
TYMSETs. Each “FRIDAY” DAYSEG could have a different set of 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  e-terratransmission Modeling Tasks 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
201
 
TYMPTs. Similarly, in the example of the DAYSEG called by the 
ABDATE, the TYMPTs for each of the DAYSEGs called “JULY4” could 
be different. The point here is that the data structure encourages you 
to use a consistent DAYSEG naming convention that makes it easier 
to design and track your schedules. 
• TYMPT (Time Point) records are t he time points, which specify the 
times when the component’s value changes. The acceptable values for 
TYMPTs lie between 00:00 and 23:59. They are children of DAYSEGs, 
and they must be entered in chronological order, from the earliest time 
to the latest. The first TYMPT in a DAYSEG that violates chronological 
sequence is ignored, along with all the other TYMPTs that follow it. 
Validation warns you of non-chronological entries. 
• TRND (Trend) records represent cal endar dates. They are children of 
TREND TYMSETs, and they must be entered in chronological order. 
As with TYMPTs, the first TRND record that violates chronological 
order is ignored, along with all the TRND records that follow it. 
• ABDATE\* records specify “abnorma l” dates or holidays that have 
abnormal patterns. They are used to “override” a day, or a portion of a 
day, in a CYCLE TYMSET. 
Each ABDATE record contains an identifier or name, the abnormal 
date, the time span (TSTART and TSTOP) that is overwritten on that 
date, and the name of the DAYSEG to be used on that date in place of 
the one normally used. ABDATE records also have an 
UPDATE/NOUPDATE flag, which is used to indicate whether or not 
the model should be updated with real-time data during the abnormal 
period specified. 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  e-terratransmission Modeling Tasks 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
202
 
 
 
Figure 77: Load-Based Regulation Schedule 
9.2 Defining and Building a Station Group Load Schedule 
Load area schedules are a set of time-varying distribution factors used to 
break down values of total system load into individual substation (busbar) 
loads. 
The modeler can define two kinds of schedules: constant schedules and 
time-dependent schedules. Constant schedules assume that the 
LDAREA’s load shape conforms to the load shape of its parent LDAREA. 
However, time-dependent schedules allow the modeler to represent 
“non-conforming” loads. Time-dependent schedules follow the load shape 
of the LDAREA, and do not conform to the load shape of the parent 
LDAREA. 
These are the options: 
• If a constant schedule applies, a LDAREA is modeled as a constant 
fraction, in proportion to its parent LDAREA’s MW load. 
• If a time-dependent schedule is requi red, the LDAREA’s MW load is 
modeled as a time-dependent fraction of its parent LDAREA’s MW 
load. 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  e-terratransmission Modeling Tasks 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
203
 
To build a constant load area schedule, complete these steps: 
1. Select the Schedules option on the View menu. 
The Auxiliary Tables view appears. 
2. Open the Schedules folder to reveal the LOAD_AREA_SCHEDULES 
item. 
3. Open the LOAD_AREA_SCHEDULES branch and open the Timeset 
Definitions folder. 
4. Position the cursor on the Timese t Definitions folder icon and click the 
right mouse button. 
5. Select the Add TYMSET  option in the pop-up menu. 
The Timeset Definition form appears in the right pane. 
6. Name the timeset, specify the Cycl e assignment, and save the record. 
7. Select the Day Segment tab and add a DAYSEG record in the Day 
Segment table. 
8. Name the record and save the entry. 
ModelerUI will prompt users if they start other entry work before saving 
the previous entry. 
9. Add TYMPT records in the Time Break Points table. 
10. Select the Week Segments tab and insert the WKSEG record. 
11. Position the cursor on the Schedules folder icon and click the right 
mouse button. 
12. Select the Add SKED option in the pop-up menu. 
The Network Schedules form appears in the right pane. 
13. Name this new schedule, and specify which timeset the schedule will 
follow. 
14. Click the Build Schedules button. When the Validation window 
appears, click the Start button in the Validation window menu bar. 
15. When the Forecast Scheduling process completes, review the results 
for errors that indicate the schedule you created might be faulty, then 
dismiss the Validation window. 
The Forecast Scheduling process provides one schedule value 
(VALUE) record with each forecast schedule created. 
Note: The screen refresh to display this item may take a few moments. 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  e-terratransmission Modeling Tasks 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
204
 
16. Save your work.

9.3 DC Circuits 
A DC circuit is an interconnected collection of DC nodes, DC lines, and 
DC converters. Multiple DC circuits within the entire network model are 
supported. 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  e-terratransmission Modeling Tasks 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide

Proprietary – See Copyright Page  e-terratransmission Modeling Tasks

Figure 79: DC Network 
9.3.1.1 Definitions 
• DC Controller: Refers to a strategy for distributing a single power 
regulation setpoint to one or more poles, or to a device for 
implementing the strategy. 
• DC Pole: A user-defined collection of one or more interconnected 
converters. A pole exists between two nodes, and it is connected to 
DC lines and other poles only at those two nodes. 
• DC Converter: An AC-DC conversion device, consisting of one or 
more bridges that are connected to the AC network by a single 
modeled transformer. 
9.3.1.2 DC Topology 
DC ground returns must be modeled explicitly using the DC line model. 
DC line records representing ground return are flagged as such. 
Circuit breakers and switches in the DC network are not supported, 
although DC Poles, DC Converters, and DC lines can be individually 
removed from service. Poles and converters removed from service can be 
either blocked (open-circuit) or bypassed (short-circuit). 
 
9.3.1.3 DC Controllers 
DC controllers deal in power setpoints only. They get their power setpoints 
either from manual entry, from the definition of a contingency being 
analyzed, or from an assigned power schedule. 
9.3.1.4 DC Poles 
A DC pole has two connections in the DC network: a single neutral 
connection, and a single “top” or “bottom” connection to one or more DC 
lines. Between its neutral connection and its DC line connection, a DC 
pole is modeled as a collection of DC converters in parallel, in series, or in 
both configurations. Within a pole, all series and parallel converter 
topological configurations are supported, including combinations of series 
and parallel converters. Topology changes can be made online by 
changing component node assignments. 
Unbalanced operation of bipoles is supported. There are no distinctions 
made in the model between “top” and “bottom” poles in a bipole, nor are 
there any restrictions against monopolar operation. 
Each pole regulates current, voltage, power, or firing/extinction angle, 
depending on its regulation mode. Poles regulate to their individual 
setpoint, which may be derived from a pole measurement (State Estimator 
only), from a schedule, or from its share of the DC controller’s setpoint. In 
the network model, each pole that is regulating power or current is 
assigned to one, and only one, DC controller. 
All poles in constant power mode must get their power setpoint from pole 
power measurement (state estimator only), from their assigned DC 
controller, from their assigned MW schedule, or (optionally) from a 
user-entered override setpoint on the pole. If from a DC controller, the 
pole will be assigned a portion of the DC controller’s power setpoint, 
based on the pole’s participation factor. 
Within the DC network solution software, all actual regulation is done at 
the pole level. Individual converters within a pole do not need to be 
assigned their own regulation setpoints or regulation modes, either by the 
user or internally by the software. At the solution, each converter’s solved 
voltage and current values are derived from its pole’s solved voltage and 
current values, distributed to converters based on converter topology 
within the pole. 
9.3.1.5 Rules for the Selection of Pole Regulation Modes 
To avoid unsolvable or unrealistic problems, the following rules must be 
followed regarding the selection of regulation modes for DC poles: 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  e-terratransmission Modeling Tasks 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
207
 
• In every DC circuit, there must be at least one pole that is regulating 
voltage or angle. However, multiple poles regulating voltage or angle 
cannot be connected in parallel (that is, to the same nodes). 
• Each pole that is regulating current or power must be a part of at least 
one loop in the DC circuit containing no other poles in that mode (a 
loop can include ground return). 
• In any pole that is regulating an gle, and whose taps are discretely 
regulating voltage, all converter transformers in the pole must use the 
same tap model. That is, they must have the same nominal tap setting, 
the same tap step size, etc., since taps in this pole will move together. 
This requirement applies only to poles regulating both angle and 
voltage. 
9.3.1.6 DC Converters 
DC converters are modeled as station components. Each converter 
consists of one or more bridges. The number of bridges in a converter can 
be changed online and will impact the nominal voltage of the converter. 
Converter nominal voltage per bridge is entered during the modeling 
phase. 
Converters cannot control independently. The concept of regulation 
modes for individual converters does not apply. Regulation modes are 
applied to poles during the solution process, and solved pole voltage and 
current levels are distributed to converters within the pole. 
No distinctions are made in the modeling or the software between 
inverters and rectifiers, except when determining angle limits. Different 
limits are supported for firing angle and extinction angle. 
The DC converter transformer model supports both two-winding 
transformers and a three-winding transformer model, with a synchronous 
condenser at the tertiary. For each converter, a single two- or 
three-winding transformer will connect it to the AC network. 
Three-winding converter transformers are modeled as three two-winding 
transformers in a Y configuration, with only the primary tap being movable. 
This configuration also supports a synchronous condenser connected to 
the tertiary. 
Movable taps on converter transformers cannot be used to regulate any 
AC bus voltages, but they can be used to regulate DC voltage, current, or 
MW by controlling the AC voltage input to the converter. 
Violations of DC converter current ratings will cause warning messages to 
be logged on the application message log. Only one set of converter 
current ratings is supported. 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  e-terratransmission Modeling Tasks 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
208
 
9.3.1.7 Distributing Solution Values from Pole to Converter 
Although converters are individually modeled in the network model, they 
are not represented individually in the internal DC network solution, where 
only poles and DC lines are represented. A solution is obtained for the DC 
network by considering only the DC lines and poles, with the poles 
modeled as pure sources (of current, voltage, etc.). Once the pole voltage 
and current values are solved for, they will be distributed to the converters 
in the pole using the following rules: 
• Voltage: A pole voltage will be split among the series configuration 
converters in proportion to the converter’s nominal voltages. A 
bypassed converter, and any converters in parallel with a bypassed 
converter, will always be assigned zero voltage. 
• Current: A pole current will be split among parallel configuration 
converters in proportion to the converter’s current ratings. 
9.3.1.8 Defining DC Converters 
To model DC converters for a substation, complete the following steps: 
1. Click the Equipment  toolbar button.

b. Model voltage level information.

substation, and expand it. 
b. Expand the DC Converters fol der to expose its contents.

Figure 80: DC Converter Definition 
9.3.1.9 DC Lines 
A DC line is used to transfer DC power from one location to another 
location, just like the AC line does. A DC line must be specified by its 
“from” and “to” DC nodes, and their characteristic resistance. The name of 
the DC node is 8 characters long, and it must be unique across DC nodes. 
The resistance of a DC line is specified in ohms, while the resistance of 
every other network element (including converter transformers) is 
specified in percent on a 100-MVA base. 
DC lines cannot be tie-lines and they cannot cross area boundaries. You 
can model all DC lines within one area, and create pseudo-substations 
that contain the DC converters. These pseudo-substations can then be 
connected to the real stations across area boundaries with zero 
impedance branch (ZBR) tie-lines. 
9.3.1.10 Defining DC Lines 
To model DC lines for a substation, complete the following steps: 
1. Click the Line toolbar button. 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  e-terratransmission Modeling Tasks 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide

2. Navigate to the appropriate substation in the Transmission Lines 
folder, and expand it. 
3. Expand the HVDC Transmission Lines folder to expose its contents. 
a. To add a new DC line record, place the cursor on the HVDC 
Transmission Lines folder, click the right mouse button, and select 
the Add DC Line option. 
b. To update an existing DC line record, select its icon. 
4. Model DC line information in the HVDC Transmission Line form. 
9.3.1.11 Defining Other DC Components 
DC Controllers, Control Blocks, Control Functions, and DC Poles are 
modeled using the DC Components tree of the Related Tables view. 
To model a DC Controller, open the DC Components tree to reveal the DC 
Controllers folder and complete these steps: 
1. Place the cursor on the DC Controller folder, click the right mouse 
button, and select the Add DCTL option. 
2. Model the controller ramp rate and MW schedule (if any) using the DC 
Controller form. 
To model a DC Control Block, open the DC Components tree to reveal the 
DC Control Blocks folder and complete these steps: 
1. Place the cursor on the DC Control Blocks folder, click the right mouse 
button, and select the Add DCTLBL option. 
2. Model the control block target value, control function, and blocking 
details using the DC Control Blocks form. 
To model a DC Control Function, open the DC Components tree to reveal 
the DC Control Functions folder and complete these steps: 
1. Place the cursor on the DC Control Functions folder, click the right

2. Model the Measurement Node assignment, DC Controller assignment,

To model a DC Pole, open the DC Components tree to reveal the DC 
Poles folder and complete these steps:

and select the Add POLE option. 
2. Model the network topology info rmation, Participation Factor, and 
schedule and regulation details using the DC Poles form.

information is from SCADA. 
− Map System Voltage Reduction Info from SCADA: When 
selected, this check box specifies that the system voltage reduction 
information is from SCADA. 
• Transaction Direction 
− Sale: Sales transactions can be designated “D” for Deliver, “S” for 
Sale, or “-”. 
− Purchase: Purchase transactions can be designated “R” for 
Receive, “P” for Purchase, or “+”. 
9.9 PSSE, IEEE, and CIM/XML Data Sets 
PSSE Power Flow Data format and IEEE Common Data format are two of 
the most widely used industry-standard data formats. e-terramodeler 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  e-terratransmission Modeling Tasks 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
216
 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  e-terratransmission Modeling Tasks 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
217
provides a utility called “PSAImport” that imports these data into the 
PowerModel database and HABITAT modeling clones. This utility 
interfaces with the Batch Loader tool that creates and implements the 
import transactions using the ADE Controls API. 
The PSSE and IEEE data formats evolved over time as the standard for 
the exchange of power flow data among power system utilities and 
vendors. 
An industry-leading implementation of the Common Information Model 
(CIM) is delivered with each release of the e-terramodeler modeling 
system. The CIM XML Import/Export is used both for creating the export 
XML files and for importing CIM XML files into e-terramodeler. 
 
 
10. Network Data Import 
This chapter provides details about PSSE and IEEE data block 
architecture, and the data propagation paths that move the PSSE and 
IEEE data from the e-terramodeler PowerModel database into the 
subsystem HABITAT databases. 
10.1 PSSE-26™ Data Blocks 
The PSSE-26 data structure consists of 16 sections of records, with each 
section containing a particular type of data required in power flow work. A 
comma or a blank separates the data items. Each section of data, except 
the case identification data, is terminated by a record value of zero. 
The 16 data blocks must maintain this order: 
• Generators 
• Branches 
• Transformer Adjustment 
• Area Interchange 
• Two-Terminal DC Lines 
• Switched Shunt 
• Transformer Impedance Correction Tables 
• Multi-Terminal DC Lines 
• Multi-Sectional Line Groupings 
• Zone 
• Inter-Area Transfer 
• Owner 
• FACTS Control Device 
Each section of record data begins with a section header. 
The mapping tables below indicate the intermediate mappings that are 
required to translate the Bus model of the PSSE to the PowerModel 
database. 
10.2 IEEE Data Blocks 
The IEEE data structure consists of six sections. Each section is 
terminated by a record of value zero. 
The six data blocks must maintain this order: 
• Title 
• Busses 
• Branches 
• Loss Zone 
• Interchange 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  Network Data Import 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
218
 
• Tie-Lines 
Each section of record data begins with a section header.

indicated below:

10.5 CIM XML Import 
By representing the common features and attributes of a power grid in 
XML format, modeling engineers can exchange system operations data 
with other CIM-compliant sites. 
The CIM is a vendor-neutral information model for representing data 
maintained in an EMS. The CIM defines the meaning or semantics of 
data, not the data’s physical representation. 
The NERC CIM XML format defines a text file representation of data in a 
CIM-inspired schema, based on XML and the Resource Description 
Framework (RDF). 
The import process is a two-step procedure and is managed through a 
Perl script, “ADECimImport.pl”. The import script (ADECimImport.pl) 
initially calls a converter program (ADECimXml.exe) that translates the 
CIM/XML data into a format compatible with the e-terramodeler Batch 
Loader. The import script then runs the Batch Loader to import the data 
into the e-terrahabitat databases via the e-terramodeler PowerModel 
database (see Table 3 below for the propagation pattern that the batch 
loading software uses when importing and distributing data to the 
e-terraplatform subsystem HABITAT databases). 
The export process is managed via the ADEUtility.exe command-line 
application, which queries the CIM server and generates a translation of 
the model in CIM/XML format. 
All of the configuration files used by CIM XML Import/Export are located in 
the Model Server/bin directory of the ADE binary distribution. The Model 
Server/bin directory will be referred to as “%ADE_ROOTDIR%” throughout 
this description. 
During the execution of the CIM Import process, two log files are 
generated that detail the progress: errors and warnings. The first of these 
files is generated by the converter program, and it will be referenced as 
“%CONV_LOG%”. The second log file is generated by the Batch Loader 
program, and it will be designated as “%BL_LOG%”. When the converter 
program is run, it creates several CSV files in addition to the log file. The 
path of this directory can be specified and will be designated as 
“%CSVDIR%”. 
All of these environment variables can be designated for creation at 
system startup, or specified interactively during run time. 
To run the CIM Import from the command line into the “%IMPORTURL%” 
context, issue the following command: 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  Network Data Import 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
234
 
perl ADECimImport.pl -x %CIMURL% -f %CIMXML -n %CIMNS% -c 
%IMPORTURL% -o %CSVDIR%

10.6 CIM/XML Export 
The ADE context URL of the export target with a CimServer will be 
designated as “%CIMURL%”, and the context URL of the import target will 
be designated as “%IMPORTURL%”. The namespace of the CIM Server 
will be designated as “%CIMNS%”. If the namespace of the CIM Server is 
unknown, run SchemaBrowser.exe on the “%CIMURL%” context to find 
the appropriate namespace. 
During the execution of the CIM Export process, a CIM/XML file is 
generated that captures a snapshot of the source database. This file will 
be referenced as “%CIMXML%”. 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  Network Data Import 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
235
 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  Network Data Import 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
236
To run an export of the CIM Export from the command line using the 
“%CIMURL%” context, issue the following command: 
ADEUtility.exe %CIMURL% -cim %CIMNS% -output %CIMXML% 
 
 
11. e-terrageneration Modeling Tasks 
This chapter provides information about the modeling tasks that specify 
the operating parameters of the e-terrageneration subsystem functions 
such as Automatic Generation Control (AGC), Operating Area Frequency 
control, and NERC reporting, and that delimit the operating ranges and 
modes of the plant equipment. 
11.1 Modeling an Automatic Generation Control (AGC) Area 
An “operating area” is defined in the EMS as an Automatic Generation 
Control (AGC) area. Operating areas are at the top of a hierarchical tree 
that is usually associated with the central business unit, the utility. 
At least one operating area must be designated as the EMS operating 
area. All other operating areas must be designated as non-EMS (external) 
areas. 
Note: Consider AGC area ordering carefully. While random order is 
allowed, it is more desirable to insert internal operating areas ahead of 
external operating areas in the e-terrageneration subsystem schema. 
Once the operating area order has been built, it cannot be changed 
without losing the structures built under each area definition. 
Whenever you define an operating area, you need to define at least one 
generating unit, so that power exchanges can be represented and 
calculated properly by the e-terrageneration and e-terratransmission 
applications, which account for power transactions by adjusting unit 
output. In addition, you must define an area frequency measurement for 
the EMS operating area. 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  e-terrageneration Modeling Tasks 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
237
 
 
 
Figure 82: Operating Area Definition Form 
11.1.1 ACE Deadband and Control Regions 
Figure 83 shows how the Area Control Error (ACE) deadband and the 
various AGC control regions are defined. The deadband and control 
regions (regulation region indicator, RRI) are a function of the relative 
values of ACE and the integral of ACE.  In addition, the ACE deadband is 
dynamic, being a function of time. 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  e-terrageneration Modeling Tasks 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
238
 
 
 
Figure 83: ACE Deadband and AGC Control Regions 
The dynamic attribute of the ACE deadband is used to adjust the effective 
deadband for AGC controls. As soon as ACE comes within the static 
deadband range, the dynamic deadband is increased to its maximum 
value, known as the “ACE emergency assist limit”.  As long as ACE stays 
within the static deadband range, the dynamic deadband equals the 
emergency assist level. If ACE comes out of the static deadband range 
and stays out, the dynamic deadband will gradually decay from the 
emergency assist level to the static deadband level. The rate of decay is a 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  e-terrageneration Modeling Tasks 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
239
 
function of the analyst-entered dynamic deadband time constant, as 
Figure 84 illustrates: 
 
 
Figure 84: ACE Deadbands and ACE Region Limits 
The dynamic ACE deadband is defined as follows: 
DACEDB = ACEDB + (DT/DTMAX) * (ACEEMN – ACEDB) 
where: 
• DACEDB is the dynamic ACE deadband. 
• ACEDB is the static ACE dead band defined in the database. 
• DT is DTMAX minus the time (in seconds) for which ACE has 
remained outside DACEDB (limited between zero and DTMAX). 
• DTMAX is the maximum time limit (in seconds) defined in the 
database. 
• ACEEMN is the emergency-assist ACE level defined in the database. 
Also: 
If the magnitude of ACE < ACEDB 
then: 
Set DT = DTMAX, or DACEDB = ACEEMN 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  e-terrageneration Modeling Tasks 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
240
 
The integral of ACE is internally limited to a maximum value, whereas 
ACE itself is not. 
11.1.2 Variable Frequency Bias Calculation 
Variable frequency bias (VFBIAS) predicts how much the MW output of an 
operating area will change for every 0.1 HZ of frequency variation that 
occurs in one AGC cycle. Valid values of VFBIAS reside between the 
variable frequency bias low limit (VFBMIN) and the high limit (VFBMAX). 
Variable frequency bias is calculated as follows: 
VFBIAS = FBLOAD + FBGEN 
where: 
• FBLOAD represents the Load Cont ribution and is calculated as 
follows: 
FBLOAD = LDBIAS /1000 * SLOAD 
where LDBIAS represents the Area Load Bias, and SLOAD represents 
the System Load. 
• FBGEN represents the Generation Contribution and is calculated as 
follows: 
FBGEN = DBV * DSK * SGVRES 
where DBV represents the Deadband Variable.  DBV represents how 
the unit governors respond to the frequency deviation exceeding a 
threshold. 
DBV values reside between the unit contribution minimum (DBVMIN) 
and maximum (DBVMAX). 
The term “DSK” represents the Droop Scalar constant. This constant 
specifies how much of the generation frequency bias will be applied to 
the area frequency bias. 
DSK is used to globally adjust the droop characteristics of all 
generators.  DSK also models how the area’s unit governors will 
respond to frequency deviations. 
SGVRES represents the sum of all responses from generators that are 
online and can respond in the direction of the frequency error without 
violating the unit regulation limit. 
In the calculation of FBGEN, the value of DBV changes with transitions of 
the absolute Actual Frequency Deviation value (AFD) into the VFBIAS 
deadband region. The change in the DBV value is determined by the 
transition scalar, PINC.  This scalar specifies the change in governor 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  e-terrageneration Modeling Tasks 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
241
 
response as the frequency deviation continues to be greater than the 
threshold. 
PINC affects DBV values as follows: 
When absolute AFD exceeds the VFBIAS deadband minimum (VFBDB1), 
then: 
DBV = min (DBVMIN, (DBV – PINC)) 
When absolute AFD drops below the VFBIAS deadband maximum 
(VFBDB2), then: 
DBV = max (DBVMAX, (DBV + PINC)) 
11.2 e-terrageneration Alarms 
Generation alarms can be issued by various tasks, as specified in the data 
structure set up by a programmer or engineer. 
When defining Generation alarms, the following three components must 
be considered: 
• Alarm characteristic definition in GENMODEL 
• Alarm specification in the ALARM database 
• Alarm format picture de finitions in RTGEN.ALMMSG 
The request to issue an alarm originates in RTGEN, using the data 
defined in GENMODEL.  The Alarm application uses ALARM database 
data to process the application and display the corresponding message 
defined in RTGEN.ALMMSG. 
How a Generation alarm is set up depends on whether the alarm needs to 
be acknowledged individually, acknowledged as part of a group, or simply 
shown on the System Activity Log (SYSACT). 
• Alarms that need to be acknowledged individually must be set up with 
a one-to-one relationship between the GENMODEL definition and the 
ALARM database definition. 
• Alarms that need to be acknowledged as a group, but not individually, 
must be set up using one of the following methods: 
− Grouping alarm exceptions under an OBJDEF record in the ALARM 
database defined for group acknowledgement. Normally, this is the 
preferred approach. 
− Assigning the same alarm ex ception ID (from the ALARM 
database) to more than one Generation alarm. 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  e-terrageneration Modeling Tasks 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
242
 
• Operator entry logging alarms are simply logged in SYSACT. 
For detailed information about ALARM database definitions and 
procedures, see the Alarm Programmer’s Guide. 
11.2.1 Modeling Generation Alarms 
To model generation alarms, complete the following steps: 
1. Select the e-terrageneration Alarms option from the View menu. 
The Generation Alarms view displays. 
2. Expand the Alarm Definitions folder  (in the Generation Alarms View 
folder) to expose its contents. 
3. Place the cursor on the Alarm Defi nitions folder, click the right mouse 
button, and select the Add ALMTSK option. 
4. Model generation alarms info rmation in the fields provided.
