11.4 Combined Cycle Unit Definition 
The Combined Cycle Unit definition describes the deployment of one or 
more combustion turbines with one generic steam turbine in a Combined 
Cycle group. This deployment is defined by specifying combined cycle 
turbine types in the Generating Unit definition, by specifying the Steam 
Turbine control scheme and by denoting which Plant Controller(s) the 
turbines are assigned to. 
11.5 Defining a Combined Cycle Group 
The database builder defines Combined Cycle groups (combustion and 
steam turbine deployment) in the Power Equipment view of ModelerUI. 
To define a Combined Cycle Group, complete these steps: 
1. Open the Generating Un it folder of the substation (plant) where the 
units reside. 
2. Specify which PLCs are deployed at the plant. 
3. Establish the Combined Cycle Group by inserting one Generating Unit 
record for each unit in the group, and by assigning the combustion 
turbine and steam turbine Plant Controllers. 
4. Specify which generating units are deployed as combustion turbines in 
the group using the Turbine Type dropdown menu on the Types tab. 
5. Specify which unit is the steam  turbine using the Turbine Type 
dropdown menu on the Generating Unit Types tab. 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  e-terrageneration Modeling Tasks 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
244
 
6. Save the Combined Cycle Group definition. 
Note: The Combined Cycle Unit model plays a significant role in the 
Dispatcher Training Simulation (DTS), and it is stored in the 
e-terrasimulator database’s CCYC record structure. This 
simulation-specific structure is built using the Combined Cycle Modeling 
view of ModelerUI, and it stores setpoint and MW output information for 
the combustion turbines. Refer to the chapter e-terrasimulator Modeling 
Tasks for details about the CCYC record structure. 
11.6 Defining the Steam Turbine Incremental Heat Rate Curve 
The output of the steam turbine in a Combined Cycle group will be a 
function of the combined combustion turbine outputs. The steam turbine 
will follow its assigned incremental heat rate curve in order to meet its 
output requirement. 
Incremental heat rate curves (IHRCs) are constructed as a series of points 
that specify the incremental heat units per MWH required to maintain 
output at an MW level. 
• Accuracy increases if numerous rate points (RATEPT) are modeled, 
especially in portions of the curve where the slope changes rapidly. 
• The amount of heat required to ma intain synchronous generation must 
be defined at the first point of the curve. 
• The first rate point MW must be 0.   Each rate point must have an MW 
value. 
• Incremental heat rates must increase for successive points between 
the MW level of the economic dispatch minimum and the maximum 
capacity. 
• Set the boiler efficiency to 100 for each unit that is not economically 
dispatchable. 
A unit is “economically dispatchable” if an IHRC can be defined for it 
and assigned to it, and if the generation type is specified as 
economically dispatchable. 
To specify the incremental heat rate curve for the steam turbine in the 
Combined Cycle group, complete these steps: 
1. Find the group’s steam turbine in the Power Equipment view, and open 
the Incremental Rate Curves tab. 
2. Specify the steam turbine MW  output based upon the combined CT 
output (see sample tables below). 
3. Save the Incremental Heat Rate Curve definition. 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  e-terrageneration Modeling Tasks 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
245
 
The values in the translation tables below show how the steam turbine 
MW output will “follow” the combined combustion turbine MW output of the 
Combined Cycle Unit group. The MW Level entries specify the CT MW 
output. The Incremental Heat Rate entries specify the ST MW output 
corresponding to the combined CT MWs. 
 
MW Level Incremental Heat Rate (MBTU) 
0 0 
30 0 
40 40 
100 40 
Note: In the table above, the ST maximum output is reached before the 
maximum combined output of the group’s CTs.  This is a common 
condition.

Note: The PLC regulates steam turbine output by interpolating between 
the modeled ST MW output values when the combined MW output of the 
CTs resides between the modeled MW levels. 
11.7 Defining the Steam Turbine Control Scheme 
Units in a combined cycle group may all be under the same PLC, or under 
individual PLCs.  If each unit has its own PLC, two possibilities for control 
exist: 
• Issue control to the CTs if they  are in “simple-cycle mode” (not 
contributing heat to the steam turbine), but redirect control to the ST if 
any CT is in “combined-cycle mode”. 
• Always issue control to the CTs to move them to their desired MW 
level (“boiler-following mode”). 
To specify whether a steam turbine controls the Combined Cycle Unit 
group, complete these steps: 
1. Find the steam turbine in the Power Equipment view and open the 
Types tab. 
2. Locate the Combine Cycle Unit information. 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  e-terrageneration Modeling Tasks 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
246
 
3. Select the Steam Turbine opti on from the Turbine Type dropdown 
menu. 
Notice that radio buttons in the Group Control panel are now active. 
4. Click the button for the desired control scheme: 
− Issued from ST PLC: Toggle this button ON to specify that control 
for the group will come from the Plant Controller that issues 
controls to the steam turbine. 
− Issued from CT PLCs: Toggle this button ON to specify that 
control for the group will come from the Plant Controller(s) that 
issues controls to the combustion turbines. 
11.8 Role of the Telemetered Status of the Heat Recovery 
Valve (HRV) 
Each CT in the Combined Cycle Unit group has a heat recovery valve that 
directs the flow-through heat of the gas turbine to the boiler of the steam 
turbine. In combined-cycle mode, where the CT contributes heat to the 
ST, the CT’s HRV is open (telemetered status = OPEN).  In simple-cycle 
mode, the CT’s HRV is closed (telemetered status = CLOSED). Refer to 
the section Generation Telemetry Model in this chapter to determine how 
generation telemetry for CT HRV status is modeled. 
In brief, the principal steps for modeling HRV telemetry are as follows: 
1. Copy a Measurement Group record that contains a Status Input 
definition for the Heat Recovery Valve (HRV) status to each of the 
Combustion Turbine records. 
2. Specify the card and storage loca tion (physical address) of the HRV 
status value using the Substation Telemetry definition. 
3. Map the telemetry for the HRV  status value from SCADA to 
e-terrageneration using the Generation Telemetry table. 
Note: Having the Heat Recovery Valve status telemetered is not an AGC 
requirement. Frequently, the HRV status is changed manually by the 
operator on the AGC displays. 
11.9 Connecting Area Tie Corridors and Tie-Lines 
The model builder defines the EMS tie corridor topology by inserting Tie 
Corridor (TIE) records on the Connecting Area Tie Corridors form. The 
model builder defines the transmission lines that carry the power in the 
corridor by inserting Tie-Line (TYLN) records on the same form.  The 
TYLN records must replicate lines that exist in the AC Transmission Line 
tree. 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  e-terrageneration Modeling Tasks 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
247
 
Tie-lines model the lines that physically connect AGC operating areas. A 
tie corridor represents a collection of tie-lines that are monitored together.

EMS Schema Modeling Guide

Figure 87: Dynamic Load Tie Definition 
11.9.1 OPA and AREA Record Synchronization 
Before invoking the generation network mapping facility, GENMAP, 
confirm that the AREA records in the utility’s Netmodel clone are ordered 
in the same sequence as the OPA records in the Genmodel clone. 
Synchronization of tie-line details in the real-time Generation and Network 
databases requires that these alignments match. 
The generation network mapping facility creates TLN records and inserts 
them in Netmodel under their assigned AREA record, based upon the tie 
corridor and tie-line designations it discovers in the Genmodel database. 
11.9.2 Defining the Connecting Area Utilities 
Before any tie corridor relationships can be modeled, the model builder 
must define paired utility (UTIL) records under the energy exchanging 
operating areas. 
The connecting utility (UTIL) names must be unique within each operating 
area of the Connecting Area Tie Corridors tree. Once the Connecting 
Utility records are in place, the tie corridor can be modeled by inserting 
TIE records and defining the transmission lines that reside in the corridor. 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  e-terrageneration Modeling Tasks 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
249
 
11.9.3 Defining the Tie Corridors 
Tie corridors are modeled by defining two mirror-image TIE records: one 
as a child of the “Near” Connecting Utility parent and one as a child of the 
“Far” Connecting Utility parent. These TIE records represent opposite 
ends of the same tie corridor, one end in each of the participating 
operating areas. 
Note: TIE records that define the same corridor must have the same 
name.

To model a tie-line on the Transmission Lines view, complete these steps: 
1. Determine which transmission lines are tie-lines (carrying bulk power 
between operating areas). 
2. Click the Line button in the ModelerUI toolbar. 
3. Navigate to the Transmission Line s folder by opening the Network Line 
Types folder. 
4. Open the Transmission Lines fold er, then open the Operating Area 
tree where the new LINE record will reside. 
5. Select the AC Transmission Lines folder icon using the right mouse 
button, and select the Add Transmission Line option that appears. 
The AC Transmission Lines tree for the selected area’s Operating 
Division appears with a New Transmission Line entry highlighted. 
6. Specify the name of the line using the AC Transmission Line form that 
appears in the right pane. 
Suggested naming scheme: Identify the transmission line that is a tie 
with a “T” prefix for quick reference. 
7. Create Line Segment (LN) record s using the right mouse button and 
the Add AC Line Segment option. 
Notice that the AC Line Ends (Near and Far) are inserted 
automatically. 
8. Specify the line operating details  for the line segments and line ends 
using the forms provided. 
Note: The model builder must manually enter the Near-end station, 
Far-end station, Near node, and Far node values on these forms. 
9. Save your work. 
10. If a branch end is measured, specify the branch end equipment type 
and set the SCADA check box on the Data Transfer tab ON. 
When the modeler saves the AC Transmission Line record, the 
modeling system propagates the branch end to the SCADA database 
as a SCADA DEVICE record. 
11. Specify branch end measurements using the Add MEAS option and 
the data point “add” structures. Specify AC Line End measurements 
and controls. 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  e-terrageneration Modeling Tasks 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
251
 
12. Validate the transmission line model by selecting the Expert User 
mode provided in the Validation window and by selecting the 
NETVALID option from the list of validation functions. 
13. Correct any errors, and save your work again. 
Note: Modeled transmission lines are captured as AC Transmission 
Line, AC Line Segment, and AC Line End records in the PowerModel 
database, and are propagated to the Netmodel database as LINE, LN, 
and LN2 records when the definition is saved. 
To model the same transmission line as a member of a tie corridor (TIE) 
definition, complete these steps: 
1. Select the Connecting Area Tie Corridors option on the View menu. 
2. Navigate to the connecting operating area of interest, and expand it. 
3. Model the connecting electr ic utilities as follows: 
a. Identify the operati ng areas that are connected by tie corridors and 
tie-lines. 
b. Add a utility (UTIL) record under these operating areas, one UTIL 
for each operating area connection that will be modeled. 
c. Identify each utility on a Connecti ng Electric Utility form using the 
dropdown menu. 
Note: The modeler can specify that the utility delivers power to 
itself over a tie-line (internal tie-line). 
4. Model tie corridors under the Connec ting Electric Utility record by 
inserting TIE records. Click the right mouse button and select the Add 
TIE option on the pop-up menu. 
5. Model tie corridor details on the Connecting Area Tie Corridor form by 
specifying the TIE record ID, the connecting company, and the 
operating parameters for bulk power exchange. Connecting company 
is optional. 
Note 1: This is one of a mirrored pair of TIE records that the model 
builder must create. The tie-lines in these paired TIE records have the 
same names and tie-line (TYLN) specification. The other member in 
the mirrored pair must reside under the other utility that is party to the 
bulk power exchange. 
The model builder must match the identifiers in these AC Line entries 
with those used to specify tie-lines in the tie corridors (Connecting Area 
Tie Corridor form). 
Note 2: Internal ties do not require a mirrored pair. 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  e-terrageneration Modeling Tasks 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
252
 
Note 3: Dynamic load ties do not require tie-lines (TYLN records). 
6. Model a dynamic load tie by se tting the Dynamic Load Tie check box 
ON.  Dynamic load ties do not have Tie-Line (TYLN) children. 
7. If the tie corridor is for both dy namic load monitoring and Joint-AGC 
operations, select the Dynamic Load Tie tab, and add requester- or 
supplier-specific Joint AGC information. 
8. Model tie-line details on the Lines in Corridor tab of the Connecting 
Area Tie Corridor form as follows (does not apply to dynamic load tie 
corridor modeling): 
a. Specify the Tie-Line ID in the Name box. This value must be 
duplicated in the mirrored-pair specification. 
b. Specify the line ID in the AC Transmission Line box. This value 
must match the ID of the corresponding tie-line on the 
Transmission Line view. 
c. Specify the line segment ID in the Segment box. 
d. If the tie-line is a transformer, set the Is a Line? check box OFF. 
e. If the tie corridor measurement is taken in the parent operating 
area, set the In This OPA? check box ON.  Switch the mirrored-pair 
flag of this type OFF. 
f. Specify the EMS operating area stat ion that delivers or receives the 
bulk power exchange. This value must be duplicated in the 
mirrored-pair specification. 
g. Specify the Equipment group (kV le vel) of the branch that carries 
the bulk power exchange to the station specified above. This value 
must be duplicated in the mirrored-pair specification. 
h. Specify the node name of  the station specified above. This value is 
duplicated in the mirrored-pair specification. 
i. Save the entry. 
9. Map tie-lines and tie corridors to  SCADA measurements and controls 
using the Generation Telemetry View templates. 
10. Save your work. 
11.10 Jointly Owned Units 
“Jointly owned units” (JOUs) are units owned by more than one operating 
area, with each operating area having its own share of the unit. Share 
records must be set up for all non-physical, internally operated JOUs. 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  e-terrageneration Modeling Tasks 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
253
 
This section contains background information about modeling JOUs. 
 
 
Figure 89: JOU Definition 
11.10.1 JOU Topology 
Three types of PLC-unit combinations are used to represent a single JOU 
site: 
• Physical Internally Operated JOU: This type has a physical internally 
operated JOU PLC and one physical internally operated JOU unit. This 
is modeled in the operating area (OPA) that is in control of the JOU 
and represents the actual unit. 
• Non-Physical Internally Operated JOU: This type is always paired 
one-to-one with a physical internally operated JOU.  This type is 
characterized by a non-physical internally operated JOU PLC and a 
non-physical internally operated JOU unit for each share owner. There 
must be at least two share units under each non-physical PLC, with 
one of these units representing a share belonging to the OPA that is 
operating the JOU. 
• Externally Operated JOU: This type is represented by a physical 
externally operated JOU PLC and one non-physical externally 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  e-terrageneration Modeling Tasks 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
254
 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  e-terrageneration Modeling Tasks 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
255
operated JOU unit. This type is modeled in an OPA’s topology when 
the OPA owns a share of a JOU that is operated by another company. 
The topology of internally and externally operated JOUs is illustrated in 
Figure 90 below.

Jointly owned units and their PLCs are inserted into the system topology 
just like other units and PLCs.  To specify that a PLC and its units are for a 
jointly owned unit, the PLC’s “JOU?” flag is set ON.  The Non-Physical flag 
also must be toggled to the correct value for the PLC and its units, 
depending on which one of the three types of JOUs is being modeled. 
Some requirements to follow about JOU topology are: 
• A physical internally operated JOU must always be followed by its 
corresponding non-physical internally operated JOU. 
• A physical internally operated JOU PLC has one and only one unit, 
and the unit must be a physical internally operated JOU. 
• An externally operated JOU PLC has one and only one unit, and the 
unit must be a non-physical externally operated JOU. 
• For e-terrasimulator operation, every non-physical internally operated 
JOU share unit belonging to an OPA other than the operating OPA has 
 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  e-terrageneration Modeling Tasks 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
256
to have a matching externally operated JOU modeled in the OPA of 
the share owner. 
• For e-terrasimulator operation, if an internally operated JOU resides in 
a non-EMS OPA, no matter how many share owners there are, only 
two shares have to be modeled. There should be one share for the 
EMS OPAs share, and one for the JOU operating OPA and the rest of 
the shares. 
• Externally operated JOU PLCs should be toggled so they cannot 
accept AGC setpoints or pulses. This allows a basepoint to be 
calculated with the PLC status set to “AUT”. 
11.10.2 JOU Measurement Topology 
Measurement processing and modeling for JOUs is the same as it is for 
normal units, except that the non-physical internally operated JOU 
representing the operating owner’s share has no measurement records.

11.11 Modeling Jointly Owned Units (JOUs) 
The Joint Ownership tab of the Generating Unit form is used to model 
ownership information for jointly owned generating units.  JOUs are 
generating units that are owned by more than one company. 
Important Note: Because much of the validation and mapping for JOUs is 
done conditionally, any errors found in JOU topology or data entry must be 
corrected before the generation model can be validated. The modeler 
should complete as much of the generation operations model as possible 
before entering JOUs. 
 
To model a jointly owned unit, enter details in the fields provided on the 
Joint Ownership tab of the Generating Unit form. 
Note: To enable data entry and updates to data on the unit Joint 
Ownership tab, select the JOU-PLC check box on the General Information 
tab of the Plant Controller form. Otherwise, the JOU information about the 
unit’s Joint Ownership tab is display only. 
• Jointly Owned Unit – PLC Information 
− JOU PLC: When selected, this check box specifies that there is a 
plant controller coupled with this jointly owned generating unit. 
− Treat JOU? When selected, this check box specifies that the plant 
controller will honor MW requests from joint interest owners even 
when the physical unit is not operating in a joint unit mode. 
− Internal JOU PLC? When selected, this check box specifies that 
the unit is a physical, internally operated, jointly owned unit coupled 
with a plant controller. 
− Non-Physical PLC? When selected, this check box specifies that 
the unit is a non-physical, internally operated, jointly owned unit 
coupled with a plant controller. 
− Need AUT/MAN Telemetry? When selected, this check box 
specifies that the plant controller cannot send the auto/manual 
status to the JOU share owner. 
− Data Transfer Interface 
∗ Write to SCADA: When selected, this radio button specifies 
that JOU PLC information is output as data to SCADA. 
∗ Send to RTU: When selected, this radio button specifies that 
JOU PLC information is output as controls to a remote terminal 
unit. 
• Jointly Owned Unit Information 
− Internal JOU? When selected, this check box specifies that the 
unit is a physical, internally operated, jointly owned unit. 
− Non-Physical? When selected, this check box specifies that the 
unit is a non-physical, internally operated, jointly owned unit. 
− Data Transfer Interface 
∗ Write to SCADA: When selected, this radio button specifies 
that JOU information is output as data to SCADA.

∗ Send to RTU: When selected, this radio button specifies that

• Operating Areas File Folder: When expanded, displays generating

The following parameters are required when modeling generating units: 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  e-terrageneration Modeling Tasks

259
 
• Schedules and Regulations: Parameters that affect the schedules 
defined for the unit, and the unit’s voltage regulation parameters. 
• State Estimation: Parameters describing how the unit participates in 
state estimation. 
• Network Limits: Parameters describing the basic network parameters 
such as rated MVA, participation factors, and minimum and maximum 
MW output. 
• Network Flags: Parameters describing the other basic network 
parameters, such as whether the unit is removed from service and has 
AGC simulation. 
• Generation Limits: Parameters describing the basic generation 
parameters, such as capacity, frequency deviation, and economic and 
LFC minimum and maximum values. 
• Unit Commitment Parameters: Parameters describing the unit’s 
participation in unit commitment. 
• Jointly Owned Unit Parameters: Parameters specifying if and how 
this unit is jointly owned. 
• Reserve Parameters: Parameters describing how and how much the 
unit is committed to reserves. 
• ED/Costing Parameters: Parameters describing how the unit 
participates in economic dispatch and its startup and running costs. 
• Unit Response Parameters: Parameters describing the unit’s ability 
to ramp up and down. 
• Special Operating Modes: Parameters describing any special 
operating modes in which the unit can be, such as a pumped storage 
unit or a synchronous condenser. 
• Dynamic Parameters: Parameters describing the dynamic aspects of 
the unit, such as the AVR lead/lag and delay, the damping coefficient, 
and whether the unit is part of a combined cycle group of units. 
For modeling purposes, these parameters can be classified into two 
general groups: 
• Steady-State Parameters are used to model the steady-state (all 
transients have died out) behavior of the unit. 
• Dynamic Parameters are used to model some of the long-term 
dynamic behavior of the unit, primarily for the Dispatcher Training 
Simulator (DTS).

12. e-terrasimulator Modeling Tasks 
This chapter provides information about the specifications that determine 
how the Dispatcher Training Simulator should operate. 
12.1 Overcurrent Relay Modeling 
The overcurrent relay (OCRY) model is the most complex relay model in 
e-terrasimulator.  It includes considerations for time-until-trip as a function 
of current, a method of accumulating incremental shifts in the relay disc 
device, automatic reclosure processing, and breaker action schedules. 
Electro-mechanical relay devices protect transmission lines. When the 
current in a line exceeds a minimum pickup current, a disc begins to turn. 
As long as the current in the line exceeds the pickup current, the disc 
advances incrementally until either the relay trips or the current drops 
below the pickup current. When the current in the line drops below the 
pickup current (with or without the aid of a circuit breaker trip), the disc 
unwinds at a linear rate until it reaches the zero position or until the 
current in the line once again exceeds the pickup current. 
Changes in disc position are computed on the basis of present disc 
position and current. When the current exceeds the pickup current, the 
disc position changes incrementally, according to the inverse time curve in 
Figure 96. 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  e-terrasimulator Modeling Tasks 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
273
 
 
 
Figure 96: Overcurrent Relay Inverse Time Curve Model 
The rate at which the relay disc advances is defined mathematically by 
what is known as the “inverse-time characteristic”.  The curve representing 
the inverse-time characteristic is called an “inverse-time curve”.  This 
curve is defined as follows: 
TPT = K / (AMPPT − AMPPICK)² 
where: 
• TPT is the trip point that co rresponds to the sustained current. 
• K is the inverse-time characteristic constant. 
• AMPPT is the sustained current being sensed. 
• AMPPICK is the overcurrent re lay’s user-defined minimum pickup 
current. 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  e-terrasimulator Modeling Tasks 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
274
 
The user models each overcurrent relay’s sustained current-to-trip point 
correspondence and minimum pickup current value. These user-specified 
values determine K, the constant in the inverse-time characteristic for that 
relay, as follows: 
K = (AMPPT − AMPPICK)² * TPT 
Figure 96 illustrates how a user-defined sustained current (AMPPT) and a 
minimum pickup current (AMPPICK) define a trip point (TPT).  The figure 
also shows the current (AMPTRIP) that causes an instantaneous trip. 
How long does it take the relay to trip?  That depends on the current in the 
line and the overcurrent relay’s modeled values. If the line current exceeds 
the overcurrent relay’s pickup value by only a little, the circuit breaker 
might not trip for an hour or more. On the other hand, if the current is at 
the relay’s AMPTRIP level, the breaker could trip instantaneously. 
The reset time specified for an overcurrent relay is the time it takes the 
relay to unwind from the trip position to the zero disc position. The reset 
time is used to calculate the linear rate at which the relay disc resets 
whenever the current falls below the pickup value. 
Directional overcurrent relays only increment the disc position when the 
sensed current flows in the specified direction. When current is flowing in 
the opposite direction, the disc will rewind regardless of the magnitude of 
the reverse current. 
12.1.1 Transformer Isolation Schemes 
Define transformer isolation schemes by linking the protecting switching 
devices to an overcurrent relay that has reclosure schedule. 
Figure 97 shows a hypothetical transformer connection. Three high-
voltage transmission lines lead off to other substations. These lines are 
connected to the high-side bus by three circuit breakers: the preferred 
breaker, OCCB_P; the alternate breaker, OCCB_A; and a third high-
voltage circuit breaker, OCCB_1. 
The high-voltage side of the transformer is connected to the high-side bus 
by a disconnect, OCCB_SWH.  Similarly, the low-voltage side of the 
transformer is connected to the low-side bus by a second disconnect, 
OCCB_SWL.  Two feeders lead off the low-side bus, connected by circuit 
breakers OCCB_2 and OCCB_3. 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  e-terrasimulator Modeling Tasks 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
275
 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  e-terrasimulator Modeling Tasks 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
276
High-side disconnect
Initial breaker actions:
PT0:  Open OCCB_P, OCCB_A, OCCB_1, OCCB_2, OCCB_3
Remaining isolation/reclosure actions:
PT1:  Open OCCB_SWH, OCCB_SWL
PT2:  Close  OCCB_P else close OCCB_A
PT3:  Close OCCB_1, OCCB_2  and OCCB_3
OCCB_2 OCCB_3
Low-side disconnectOCCB_SWL
OCCB_SWH
OCCB_P OCCB_A OCCB_1

Breaker
Preferred
Breaker

Figure 97: Hypothetical Transformer Isolation Scheme 
One possible sequence of events resulting in a hypothetical transformer 
isolation scheme is as follows: 
1. The relay trips, and all the break ers around the transformer except the 
high-side and low-side disconnects open (OCCB_P, OCCB_A, 
OCCB_1, OCCB_2, and OCCB_3 open).  These actions would 
correspond to the action specified at the zero time point (PTO) on the 
CB_OC display. 
 
At the first reclosure time point (specified by the first Reclosure Point 
record for this OCCB reclosure schedule), the high-side and low-side 
disconnects open (OCCB_SWH and OCCB_SWL open).  These 
actions would correspond to the action specified at reclosure point 
PT1. 
2. At the second reclosure time point, the preferred breaker (OCCB_P) 
closes if its source (line) side is energized. If the preferred breaker 
cannot close, the alternate breaker (OCCB_A) closes if its source side 
is energized. These actions would correspond to the action specified at 
reclosure point PT2 on the CB_OC display. 
Note 1: For this scheme to work as described, OCCB_P and OCCB_A 
must be defined as “LLDB”.  This ensures that the transmission line is 
energized before e-terrasimulator can close in the high-side bus. 
Note 2: If the transformer is tapped on a line, OCCB_P and OCCB_A 
must be defined as “LBDL” in NETMODEL, and the breaker node order 
definition must be correct with respect to bus and line. 
Refer to the preferred and alternate breaker’s corresponding Circuit 
Breaker records when making their assignments. 
The FROM and TO fields name the nodes to which the CB is attached. 
For e-terrasimulator, the FROM field contains the node assumed to be 
the bus side of the circuit breaker. The TO field node is assumed to be 
the source (line) side of the circuit breaker. 
3. At the third reclosure time poi nt — and only if either OCCB_P or 
OCCB_A has been successfully closed — all other breakers close.  
OCCB_SWH and OCCB_SWL remain open. These actions would 
correspond to the action specified at reclosure point PT3. 
12.1.2 Line Isolation Schemes 
If the overcurrent relay is protecting a line (LN), one of two methods can 
be used: 
• Method 1: Link the reclosure schedule to the overcurrent relay. In this 
scheme, certain circuit breakers can be specified as designated 
breakers. When the relay trips, all the breakers trip first. The reclosure 
schedule is first activated for the designated breakers. Once the 
designated breakers close successfully, the reclosure scheme is 
activated for the other breakers. There is a two-second delay between 
activations. 
• Method 2: Link a unique reclosure schedule to each of the breakers 
under overcurrent relay control. This allows for master and slave 
breakers to be specified, where a slave begins its reclosure sequence 
only after its master has successfully reclosed. There is also a user-
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  e-terrasimulator Modeling Tasks 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
277
 
specified delay between the end of the master’s schedule and the 
activation of the slave’s schedule. 
12.1.3 Defining Overcurrent Relays 
To model overcurrent relays for a substation, complete the following steps: 
1. Click the Equipment  toolbar button. 
2. Navigate to the Power Equipment folder for the appropriate substation, 
and expand it. 
3. Expand the Overcurrent Relays folder (in the Protective Devices folder) 
to expose its contents. 
a. To model a new overcurrent re lay, place the cursor on the 
Overcurrent Relays folder, click the right mouse button, and select 
the Add OCRY option. 
b. To update an existing overcurr ent relay, select its icon. 
4. For each breaker controlled by t he relay, model relay parameters and 
relay responses in the fields provided. 
 
 
Figure 98: Overcurrent Relay Definition 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  e-terrasimulator Modeling Tasks 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
278
 
12.1.4 Defining Breaker Reclosure Schedules 
The material in this section describes line reclosure sequences, but it can 
also be applied to other reclosure schemes. 
If a relay monitoring a transmission line trips, a multi-attempt reclosure 
sequence is typical. The first reclosure attempt is almost instantaneous, 
the next occurs after 10 or 15 seconds, and another occurs approximately 
45 seconds after the relay trips. 
You can model many different reclosure schemes. Specifying reclosure 
actions for several time points is entirely optional. Multiple time points are 
typically used to model complicated reclosure schemes. 
For complex bus arrangements, such as a breaker-and-a-half or a 
double-breaker, the first breaker to be reclosed can be designated; this 
designated breaker is always processed first. In reclosure sequences, only 
the designated breaker is processed. The designated breaker is 
processed until it successfully closes. The non-designated breakers are 
then closed simultaneously, two seconds after the designated breaker 
closes. 
 
 
Figure 99: Overcurrent Breaker Association

12.1.4.2 Hypothetical Isolation Scheme 
Any reclosure schedule used by an overcurrent relay to isolate a 
transformer must have at least three reclosure points in the schedule to 
allow: 
• High-side disconnect opening. 
• Reclosure of a preferr ed or alternate breaker. 
• Reclosure of all other break ers except low-side disconnect. 
For an example of a transformer isolation/reclosure scheme, see 
Transformer Isolation Schemes.

2. Add Breaker Reclosure Schedu les records under the Breaker 
Reclosure Schedules folder. 
3. Enter data on the Breaker Reclosure Schedules form. 
12.2 Voltage Relays 
Voltage relays monitor voltage at a specified node. If the voltage violates 
the user-specified maximum or minimum for a period longer than the 
user-specified delay time, any breakers controlled by that relay will be 
brought to one of three designated states: open, closed, or no change. 
You can specify that the relay be locked out after it trips. If the relay is 
locked out, the dispatcher cannot take any action on the relay or any of 
the breakers it controls, and the relay is inoperative for the user-specified 
lockout time. 
12.2.1 Defining Voltage Relays 
To model voltage relays for a substation, complete the following steps: 
1. Click the Equipment  toolbar button. 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  e-terrasimulator Modeling Tasks 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
280
 
2. Navigate to the Power Equipment folder for the appropriate substation, 
and expand it. 
3. Expand the Voltage Relays folder (in the Protective Devices folder) to 
expose its contents. 
a. To model a new voltage relay,  place the cursor on the Voltage 
Relays folder, click the right mouse button, and select the Add VRY 
option. 
b. To update an existing voltage relay, select its icon. 
4. Model voltage relay parameters a nd responses in the fields provided.

12.3 Frequency Relays 
The frequency relay model checks for under- and over-frequency in the 
system. When the frequency at a node violates the user-specified 
minimum or maximum trip frequency for a period longer than the 
user-specified delay time, any breakers controlled by that relay will be 
brought to one of three user-specified states: open, closed, or no change. 
There is an option to lock out the relay after it trips. When a relay is locked 
out, the dispatcher cannot take any action on the relay or any of the 
breakers it controls until the user-specified lockout time has elapsed. 
12.3.1 Defining Frequency Relays 
To model frequency relays for a substation, complete the following steps: 
1. Click the Equipment  toolbar button. 
2. If you are adding a new relay, verify  that the substation is enabled for 
transferring relay-modeling data to e-terrasimulator. 
For an existing frequency relay, continue from step 3.

substation, and expand it.

form.

for Relays?) option is selected. 
3. Navigate to the Power Equipment folder for the appropriate substation, 
and expand it.

12.4 Generic Under-Frequency Relays 
The generic under-frequency relay model is defined at a company level, 
and it is used to simulate loadshed by scaling a company’s load as a 
function of frequency. This relay model does not replace, but augments, 
the existing over/under-frequency relay model. Through these two models, 
the modeler can specify the under-frequency protection strategy of the 
company. 
Each relay has three frequency breakpoints and three corresponding 
load-scaling factors. When the frequency at a company drops below a 
frequency breakpoint, the loads defined for that company are scaled by 
the corresponding load-scaling factor. 
Load-scaling factors take effect independently as the frequency drops past 
the breakpoints. For example, to scale a parent company load by 10% at 
FREQ1, you would set LS1 to 0.9000.  To scale the same unadjusted 
parent load another 10% at FREQ2, you would set LS2 to 0.8100 
(0.90*0.90).  To scale parent load another 10% at FREQ3, you would set 
LS3 to 0.7210 (0.81*0.90).  In this way, the scaling factors independently 
scale the parent load. 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  e-terrasimulator Modeling Tasks 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
284
 
In e-terrasimulator, the frequency at each load point is evaluated prior to 
power flow processing.  e-terrasimulator finds which island the load point 
is in, and uses the island frequency to determine the correct load-scaling 
multiplier. This load scalar is obtained from the generic under-frequency 
relay model for the load point’s parent company. 
If the per-unit frequency is above the FREQ1 value, the multiplier is set 
to 1.0.  If the per-unit frequency is below the FREQ1 value, a load 
scalar — LS1, LS2, or LS3 — is used to reduce the load. This resultant 
load is then used in the powerflow calculation. 
Note: In e-terrasimulator, frequency is constant within an island, 
independent of company boundaries. If a company spans more than one 
island, only the company’s loads that are in the low-frequency island 
would be scaled. 
12.4.1 Defining Generic Under-Frequency Relays 
To model generic under-frequency relays, complete the following steps: 
1. Select the Generic DTS Tabl es option from the View menu. 
The Auxiliary Tables view appears. 
2. Expand the Generic DTS Tables folder to reveal the Generic 
Under-Frequency Relays folder. 
3. Add a generic under-frequency relay record, or update the existing 
record by selecting its icon. 
4. Model the generic under-fr equency relay using the Generic 
Under-Frequency Relay form. 
Note: Model the default generic under-frequency relay using the 
Default Generic Under-Frequency Relay Parameters form. 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  e-terrasimulator Modeling Tasks 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide

12.5 Synchro-Check Relays 
The synchro-check device model is used on circuit breaker closures 
initiated by the trainee or relay action. The generic under-frequency relay 
model is used to scale a company’s load as a function of the frequency 
within that company. 
Prime movers and relays are defined by operating characteristics, and by 
topology — the way in which the elem ents of the system are connected.

12.6 Static VAr Compensators 
A “Static VAr Compensator” (SVC) is defined in conjunction with the 
capacitor to which the SVC belongs. An SVC is used only in the 
e-terrasimulator environment. Each SVC name must be unique within a 
substation. 
12.6.1 Defining Static VAr Compensators 
1. Model or access one of the ex isting Shunt Reactive Devices 
(capacitors). 
2. Add a static VAr compensator re cord under the capacitor record. Use 
the form provided to specify the various parameters.

12.7 Specifying Dispatcher Training Simulator Parameters 
This section identifies those parameters that the e-terrasimulator 
instructor must define to tune up the power system simulation. 
 
 
Figure 105: e-terrasimulator Subsystem Parameters 
12.7.1 Identifying the DS (AGC-SCADA) Operating Area 
The dispatch simulation requires that an AGC-SCADA operating area 
exist in the e-terrasimulator model to simulate a neighboring utility. This 
requirement supports the simulation of power exchange and intersite data 
exchange and controls. The e-terrasimulator instructor acts as the 
SCADA dispatcher at this site and communicates in that role when the 
trainee calls to schedule/request power exchanges. 
Note: The e-terrasimulator instructor identifies the AGC-SCADA operating 
area as the “DS” site during the e-terrasimulator build operation. 
12.7.2 Peaking Unit and Unit CB Definitions 
To define the unit circuit breaker (UNCB), and to specify performance 
parameters for peaking units, use the fields provided on the DTS tab of 
the Generating Unit form as follows: 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  e-terrasimulator Modeling Tasks 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
288
 
• Circuit Breaker Assignment Window: Specify the switching device 
by navigating to the device name and selecting it. 
• Peaking Unit Parameters 
− Unit MW Ramp Target Value: MW output DTS units will ramp to 
when the simulator starts. 
− Normal Ramp Rate Factor: Percentage of AGC ramp rate. The 
simulator uses this value to determine unit ramp rate. 
− Unit Offline Threshold: Percentage of unit capacity. The simulator 
uses this value to determine whether a unit is offline. 
• Unit Breaker Table: List of switching devices assigned to the unit. Add 
a UNCB record (line) to the table by using the right mouse button to 
display the Edit menu.  For making assignments: Toggle the box to the 
left of the row of interest to select the row. 
• Assign Button: When this button is activated, clicking it associates 
the unit with the selected switching device.  For making assignments: 
With a row selected in the Unit Breaker table, open the Circuit Breaker 
assignment window and select the identifier representing the switch of 
interest. This activates the Assign button. 
• Model Unit Circuit Breakers Explicitly? A check mark specifies that 
the unit circuit breaker maps to an explicit device. 
12.7.3 Specifying Area Frequency, Load Scaling, and Cold-Load 
Pickup Parameters 
To define area frequency, load scaling, and cold-load pickup parameters 
for the simulation, use the fields provided on the DTS tab of the 
Generating Unit form as follows: 
• Exponent of Unit Frequency Affecting Real Power: The exponent of 
per-unit frequency affecting real power load. This value affects the 
DTS only. 
• Exponent of Unit Voltage Affecting Reactive Power: The exponent 
of per-unit voltage affecting reactive power load. 
• Exponent of Unit Frequency Affecting Reactive Power: The 
exponent of per-unit frequency affecting reactive power load. This 
value affects e-terrasimulator only. 
• Participate Cold-Load Pickup: When selected, this check box 
specifies that a restoration response is part of this load type. 
• Initial Time: The initial time on the cooling characteristic in seconds. 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  e-terrasimulator Modeling Tasks 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
289
 
Proprietary – See Copyright Page  e-terrasimulator Modeling Tasks 
EMS Schema Modeling Guide 
290
• Initial Scaling Factor: The scaling factor on the cooling characteristic. 
• Time to Reach Maximum Scaling: The maximum time that the load 
could be scaled by upon its reconnection to service. 
• Maximum Scaling Factor: The scaling factor used to convert the 
difference in successive pulse accumulator count readings to 
engineering units. 
• Recovery Time: The time, in seconds, needed for the load to linearly 
return to its original load pattern following cold-load pickup. 
12.7.4 Combined Cycle Unit Simulation 
The Combine Cycle Unit view in the Modeling application (see the View 
menu) provides the fields for specifying details for CCYC unit simulation. 
This section discusses concepts for modeling the throttle pressure 
setpoint whose calculation determines the behavior of the Combined 
Cycle Unit groups in the DTS. 
The basic design of a combined cycle model in the DTS is a mathematical 
link between multiple combustion turbines (GTs) and the single fossil unit 
(GST).  The total output of the GTs is used to modify an input (throttle 
pressure setpoint, TPSP) to the GST unit prime mover. The mathematical 
linkage between the total output MW of the gas turbines and the throttle 
pressure setpoint of the generic steam turbine is expressed as follows: 
Define two points: 
• Point #1 (START, TPSPI) 
• Point #2 (END, TPSPC) 
where: 
• START: This is the total minimu m GT output that has an effect on 
GST. 
• TPSPI: This is the throttle pressu re setpoint of GST with total GT 
output < START MWs. 
• END: This is the total maximum GT  output after which total GT output 
has no more effect on GST. 
• TPSPC: Throttle pressure setpoint constant. 
• TPSP: The throttle pressure setpoi nt of the GST for total GT output 
of X p.u MWs. 
The throttle pressure setpoint value (TPSP) is calculated as follows: 
() [] ()TPSP TPSPI TPSPC TPSPI
END START X START−= −
− −*1
